# Climate Change

India is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and ranks 7th on the Climate Risk Index and 31st on the INFORM Climate Risk Index, incurring losses worth $68 billion annually, due to extreme weather events.

## Proposed Interventions

### 1. Developing a National Ecosystem to Promote Climate Finance (NEPCF)

- Utilizing the past learning of SIDI, the program will set up a blended finance facility targeting the reduction of carbon emissions and increasing resilience in the MSME sector.
- The facility will provide concessional loans to MSMEs for climate-resilient technologies and services.
- It will also provide loans at concessional terms to MSMEs either directly or through Participating Financial Institutions (PFIs) to access climate finance products.

### 2. Undertake Technology Need Assessments for MSMEs

- To facilitate the MSMEs to undertake mitigation interventions, a technology need assessment is required.
- The program will conduct a technology need assessment.

### 3. Awareness Generation and Capacity Building of MSMEs

- The technology assessment will help build awareness on climate technologies and create niche market demand for their adoption among the MSMEs.
- Further, it will also provide capacity building to the MSMEs in disseminating information about low carbon and climate-resilient technologies.

### 4. Awareness Generation and Capacity Building of Participating Financial Institutions (SIDI)

- SIDI will seek to support PFIs in providing blended finance solutions for MSMEs.
- It will also build their capacity on climate risk management, particularly building and creating awareness of climate risk assessment to tackle the challenges posed by climate change.

### 5. Developing a National Ecosystem to Promote Industrial Decarbonization

- The exclusive investment in climate-friendly Clean Technologies in the industrial sector is required for two reasons: (a) New Innovation in Climate Change domain; and (b) Acceleration of the market for climate technologies.
- The program will seek to support PFIs in providing blended finance solutions for MSMEs.

### 6. Enabling Environment for a Low-Emission and Climate-Resilient MSE Sector

- The program seeks to support PFIs in providing blended finance solutions for MSMEs.

### 7. Develop and Implement MSME sectorial adoption and mitigation benchmarking tools

- The program will contribute to the upgrading and further development of the online assessment & benchmarking tools for MSMEs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 4

## Energy Efficiency
The Project will enable saving of more than 27 taks/ MWh energy. Studies indicate that the value of the productivity and operational benefits derived can be up to 2.5 times the value of the energy savings.

## Impact on Employment
The project will create approximately 10,000 direct jobs in India. Additionally, the project will create ~420,000 new jobs directly related to climate change Mitigation.

## Leveraging Private Sector Finance
The proposed project is expected to leverage USD 379 million (TA grant) potentially leveraging USD 537 million (Co-finance of USD 800 million mobilization of 2400 million plus MSME investments). The total programme outlay of USD 379.6 million. The fund will be utilized towards a dedicated investments in MSME sector and the project will also support the development of a climate-resilient MSME ecosystem. Increased concessional finance would help MSMEs in overcoming the barriers of high capital cost as well as increased competition.

## Promoting Innovation
The support from the programme to new and young companies and entrepreneurs who follow innovative business models, which may be entirely new or even challenge the business models of existing companies will unleash climate innovations, and thereby facilitate adaptation. This type of innovation has to be done especially in the case of adaptation interventions which have to be locally suited and context specific.

## Promoting women's entrepreneurship
Various TA activities such as training workshops and capacity building will be conducted so that they can become an independent entrepreneur. These initiatives will help in increasing the existing women employment ratio in MSMEs.

B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

The Indian subcontinent encompasses varied climatic zones including the north covered by Himalayan ranges. The Northwest and Northeast regions are characterized by high rainfall, which is crucial for the Indian economy and often regarded as the backbone of the agricultural sector, which employs over half of India's workforce.

However, the variability of the Indian monsoons and associated wet and dry conditions have direct impacts on socio-economic development including the gross domestic product (GDP) [Gadgil and Gadgil 2006; Wang, Gadgil, and Kumar 2006; Gulati, Saini, and Jan 2013].

### Observed and projected climate change

#### Temperature:
India's average annual mean temperature during 1951-2020 showed an increasing trend of 0.6°C/100 years with significant increasing trend in maximum temperature (0.99°C/100 years) and relatively lower increasing trend (0.24°C/100 years) in minimum temperature. Considerably greater warming of 0.14-0.25°C per decade has been experienced over the last three decades, with a particularly strong increase during winter months (0.68°C/100 years) followed by winter season (0.67°C/100 years). India's average temperature may rise by around 0.7°C during 2050-2079.

#### Eutufs:
Temperatures in India are projected to increase by +1°C under the RCP8.5 emissions scenario, and by +3°C under the RCP2.6 emissions scenario. By mid-century, projections indicate increases in annual mean temperatures of 1.5–2°C in sub-tropical regions and 1.2–2°C in tropical regions in the country. Warming is stronger in summer months (0.8-1.3°C) than winter months (+0.6°C). The projected increase in maximum temperature is 18-21% higher than the rise in average temperatures (Figure 1). Such increases are also supported by regional models used for the development of the IPCC's Sixth Assessment Report [AR6]. Under the SSP5 scenario, India's mean annual temperature may increase to +3.7°C by 2090 compared with a baseline period of 1980-2010 (Figure 2). Warming on higher emissions pathways is strongly biased towards the winter months (+4.5°C) and summer months (+3.6°C), while warming in other seasons is less pronounced as temperatures exceed the current average maximums and minimums by 10% days and nights.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V12.0 | PAGE 7

In the past several decades during the southwest monsoon, it was observed that India as a whole experienced 3 "normal" or above-normal", and 3 below-normal monsoon years. However, analysis of these trends at the district level showed that there has been a significant decrease in rainfall over most parts of India since the mid-1980s. This is reflected by a high number of excessive rainfall years.

Of the 25 districts with the highest southwest monsoon rainfall in the past decade (2012–2022), more than half have experienced a decline, compared to only about 10 per cent compared to the climatic baseline (1982-2011).

When we looked at the frequency of wet spells over India, there was not significantly continuous over forty years. We found that nearly 11 per cent of the Indian taluks (blocks) witnessed a decrease particularly in the past decade (2012–2022). This is significant because these blocks are located in the northeastern and northwestern parts of India which contribute to more than half of India's agricultural production, northeastern India, and the Indian Himalayan region. These regions have experienced a decline in rainfall over the last four decades.

In our study of localized wet rainfall extremes, we found that nearly 64 per cent of Indian taluks experienced an increase in extreme precipitation events during the past decade during the southwest monsoon. This pattern is prominent in the taluks of states with the highest GDPs.

Climate hazards and impacts

In 2023, India has witnessed over 81 natural hazards, with 83 per cent of them being hydro-meteorological. In India, in particular, incurred significant losses, primarily due to floods caused by the monsoons (WMO 2023).

### Observations

1. In monsoon-dominated regions like India, droughts have been recurrent and caused major famines in the 19th and 20th centuries.

CLOSELY linked to Indian Ocean warming and El Nino/Southern Oscillation (ENSO), India is highly vulnerable to droughts with about two-thirds of its area prone to drought Being an agricultural-dominant country and home to 1.4 billion people, droughts in India profoundly impact agricultural productivity, water resource management, and socio-economic well-being. The frequency and intensity of extreme precipitation events have increased over the past few decades, and this trend is projected to be further exacerbated by climate change. With the increasing food demand due to rising population and urbanization, the impact of droughts is expected to become more severe in the future.

![](https://www.nature.com/articles/srep13087/figures/2.png)

Evidence shows that parts of South Asia have become drier since the 1970s with an increase in the number of dry spells and a decrease in rainfall. This has led to reduced crop yields, especially in India's crop area and led to a huge fall in crop production. Droughts are expected to be more frequent in some areas, especially north-western India. Crop yields are expected to fall significantly because of extreme heat by the 2040s.

**Figure 4:** Regions in India exposed to extreme drought events and those exposed to compounded drought events

Extreme precipitation events in India in the past caused flooding, affected economy, and resulted in the loss of lives (Wassmann et al., 2015). Several extreme precipitation events occurred during the recent decades are responsible for their impact. The most severe event was the monsoon rainfall event that occurred on 18th August of the year 2005 in western states (July 2005) was a disaster that led to flooding affecting 26 million people and causing around 1200 deaths.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.01 PAGE B

## Glaciers of Himachal Pradesh

### Melting of Glaciers

According to a recent report, the glaciers in Asia's Hindu Kush Himalayas region are melting at an alarming rate due to global warming, which causes both dangerous flooding and water shortage. According to the assessment conducted by the International Centre for Integrated Mountain Development (ICIMOD), the glaciers in the region show about 65% faster than the average rate of retreat over the past century.

### Glaciers in Himachal Pradesh

- **Glaciers in Himachal Pradesh:** The glaciers in Himachal Pradesh have lost up to 7.0% of their volume, leading to significant flooding and water shortages for the approximately 240 million people who depend on them.
  
#### Factors Affecting Glaciers
- **Where westerly winter winds are the major source of moisture -** Some glaciers remain stable or even advance. On other hand, many glaciers have retreated significantly over the past century.

### Climate Change and Extreme Cyclones

Since the middle of the 20th century (1951–18), the frequency of very severe cyclonic storms during the post-monsoon season has increased significantly. During 2000-2016, the frequency of severe cyclones has been increasing at the rate of 1 every two years compared to one in five years between 1951 and 1977.

### Water Resources

The eastern part of India is highly exposed to extreme cyclone events. Further, the study highlights that the warming of oceans causes sea levels to rise, causing thermal-hydrate expansion, which intensifies the strength and frequency of tropical storms. The study also notes that there has been a significant increase in the number of extreme cyclones between 1877 and 2050; as many as 108 of these were extreme cyclones that affected a 50 km-long strip on the east coast of India.

### Conclusion

A study by National Disaster Management Institute (NDMI) highlights the findings related to climate change, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events affecting nearly half of India's coastal areas due to climate-related disasters.

25. India ranks 31st on the INFORM Risk Index, ranking losses worth $84 billion annually, due to extreme weather events.

The country has a very high absolute exposure to flooding (ranking joint 9th), including rivers, flood and droughts.

Exposure to tropical cyclones and related hazards (ranking 13th), along with drought is also high (ranking 13th). India's disaster risk is also driven by its vulnerability ranking joint 31st), which is driven by high levels of social deprivation.


26. According to ND Global Adaptation Initiative Index, the key components comprising vulnerability in India are: Ecosystem characteristics (50% of vulnerability), exposure (47% of vulnerability), and adaptive capacity (13% of vulnerability). Infrastructure (10% of vulnerability) and water (17% of vulnerability). Further, India's socio-economic characteristics comprise: poverty (28% of vulnerability), inequality (26% of vulnerability), and social exclusion (25% of vulnerability).

The global poor live in > 2 million people being without shelter. ~92 million people lacking access to safe drinking water; approximately 1 billion people lack access to electricity, and more than half a billion people are undernourished.

A large portion of the country is dependent on climate-sensitive agricultural and ecosystem-based livelihoods. According to the World Bank, India's economy has been growing at an average rate of about 7% per year since independence in 1947. However, growth in GHG emissions is not contained. Therefore, attaching impacts of climate change has a high priority for India, as clearly outlined by its NDCs submitted to the UNFCCC.


Climate Change (GHG) Emissions profile

27. As per India's Third National Communication (TNC) and Initial Adaptation Communication submitted by India to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in December 2013, India's net national emissions in 1986 were estimated at 450 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent. The country's total emissions increased to 750 million tonnes and a 115 per cent increase since 1994. Carbon dioxide continues to remain the most emitted GHG, comprising 79 per cent of all emissions in India.

The energy sector is the largest emitter followed by fossil fuels, biomass emissions from livestock and increasing aluminium and cement production are attributed to this.


Figure 6: Contribution of various sectors to GHG emission of India.


28. As per India's GHG inventory 2019, the contribution of energy sector towards the overall GHG emissions stands at 75.80 per cent followed by Agriculture sector (34.48 per cent), Industrial Process and Product Use (IPPU) sector accounts for 0.25 per cent and other sectors account for 69.17 per cent.


The Energy sector, which includes the consumption of fossil fuels in the Country and their associated fugitive emissions, is the main GHG emitting sector in the Country, contributing to 75.81 per cent of the total GHG emissions in 2019 (excluding fugitive emissions). The share of energy sector has increased by about 3 percentage points from 72.64 per cent in 2018 mainly due to the steady increase in the Country's energy consumption, including the consumption of coal and natural gas for power generation and the consumption of lignite fuels for transportation.

## Groundwater

Climate change influences groundwater systems in several ways. In terms of the hydrological cycle, climate change can affect the amounts of soil infiltration, deeper percolation, and hence groundwater recharge. Also, rising temperature increases evaporation rates which reduces the amount of water available for recharge. Climate change also affects rainfall patterns; it is rain-fed, making the country highly dependent on groundwater. Even without climate change, 15% of India's groundwater resources are overexploited. The Central Ground Water Board (CGWB) and States indicates that extraction is in excess (on an average for the entire country from 2014 to 2018).

According to the Central Groundwater Board of India, 17% of groundwater blocks are overexploited, meaning that the rate at which water is extracted is higher than the rate at which the aquifer can replenish. Groundwater resources, which support over 60 per cent of the population and provide drinking water for more than 95 per cent of urban areas and 45% of all urban areas, are under pressure from groundwater pollution and the effects of climate change.

## Impact on Food Security

In 1996 defined food security thus: "Food security exists when all people, at all times, have physical, social and economic access to sufficient, safe and nutritious food which meets their dietary needs and food preferences for an active and healthy life." The FAO defines food security as the ability of a person or population to obtain adequate amounts of food, and food absorption. Food security is one of the leading concerns associated with climate change. Climate change affects agricultural production in several ways: it can reduce crop yields; increase temperature and decrease precipitation; cause economic consequences in the form of reduced incomes, eroded livelihoods, trade disruption and adverse health impacts. Climate change also increases the vulnerability to food insecurity by increasing the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events on the underlying vulnerabilities. According to the Food and Agriculture Organization (2016), both biological and social vulnerabilities determine the net impact of climate change on food security. "Even without climate change, world food prices are expected to increase in the next decade due to population growth, urbanization, changing diets, and increased demand for biofuels." Recent studies show that wheat yields peaked in India and Bangladesh around 2001 and have not increased since then. In contrast, rice yields have continued to rise. The climate change has had a negative impact on wheat yields but has had a substantial negative effect on wheat yields, and rising temperatures may only aggravate the situation. Seasonal water availability is also expected to decline in India by 2050. This will further reduce crop yields. Should current trends persist, substantial yield reductions in both rice and wheat can be expected in the near and medium term.

By mid-century (around 2050s), the country may need to import more than twice the amount of food-grain than would be required without climate change.

# Impact on Energy Security

Climate-related impacts on water resources can undermine the two dominant forms of power generation in India - hydroelectric and thermal. Both depend on adequate water supplies to function effectively.

## Climate Change and its Impacts on Energy Security

### Hazards:
- **Increased temperatures** and heat waves.
- **More frequent droughts**, expected to be more severe in northern regions of India.

### Impact!:
- Increased heat stress-related mortality, morbidity, and hospitalization in urban areas and among agro-climatic zones.
- Reduced ground vegetation cover due to heat stress; reduced agricultural productivity where thresholds are exceeded.
- Diminished milk production due to heat stress.
- Reduced suitable ranges for species, leading to biodiversity loss because of degradation from wildfires, reducing biodiversity and livelihoods.

### Adaptation Solutions:
1. **Heat Action Plans**: Cities like Ahmedabad have developed heat preparedness plans and early warning systems; training of healthcare professionals; adjustments to urban planning to reduce heat island effects.
2. **Example of specific technological intervention: Financing** technologies that reduce water use, increase resilience of crops etc.
3. **Architectural and Urban Planning Improvements**: Using green roofs, planting trees, using reflective materials can help mitigate indoor heat; green roofs can also reduce urban heat island effects (Mahaviraj et al., 2020).
4. **Examples specific technological intervention: Financing** various climate proofing products such as improved roofing and insulation.
5. **Agricultural Adaptation**: Farmers in heat-prone areas have been trained to use drought-resistant seeds, crops resistant to heat stress; modifying irrigation practices to reduce water use; using drip irrigation systems.
6. **Enhanced Surveillance and Healthcare**: Improved surveillance and early warning systems for ensuring timely medical interventions; increasing the capacity of health facilities.

## Summary of climate hazards, its impacts and indicative adaptation solutions:

| Adaptation Solutions | Examples Specific Technological Intervention |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------|
| Heat Action Plans     | Financing technologies that reduce water use, increase resilience of crops etc. |
| Architectural and Urban Planning Improvements | Using green roofs, planting trees, using reflective materials can help mitigate indoor heat; green roofs can also reduce urban heat island effects (Mahaviraj et al., 2020). |
| Agricultural Adaptation | Farmers in heat-prone areas have been trained to use drought-resistant seeds, crops resistant to heat stress; modifying irrigation practices to reduce water use; using drip irrigation systems. |
| Enhanced Surveillance and Healthcare | Improved surveillance and early warning systems for ensuring timely medical interventions; increasing the capacity of health facilities. |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 PAGE 14

## Drought

- **Dependent on forests**: Forests help slow glacial melt in the short term but are vulnerable to flooding and widening fires.
- **Reduced coverage of glaciers due to glacial melt to forest fires**: This leads to long-term, reduced water availability for irrigation and hydropower.
- **Increased heat-related illnesses and increased prevalence of diseases such as diarrhea and malaria**.

## Increased frequency and intensity of heavy precipitation events

- **Increased flood-related mortality and morbidity**, with an increase in gastrointestinal disease from drinking water quality.
- **Degraded crop and pasture land from flooding**: By the 2040s, extreme rainfall could affect an additional 3.1–34 million people worldwide.
- **Increased incidence of landslides**.

## Urban Planning and Infrastructure Improvements

- Enhancing drainage systems in urban areas to handle larger volumes of runoff during heavy rain events.
- Using permeable materials in urban infrastructure to withstand heavy rainfall and allow water infiltration into the ground (Kristjánsson et al., 2010).
- **Technological intervention**.

## Early Warning Systems and Forecasting

Developing early warning systems for extreme rainfall events more accurately, providing crucial lead time for emergency response. Developing high-resolution models to better predict monsoon patterns and rainfall intensity is essential.

## Agricultural Practices

Farmers are encouraged to adopt crop diversification strategies to manage water resources better. Crop diversification and the use of drought-resistant crops can mitigate the effects of both excess and insufficient rainfall (Barke & Mathi, 2013).

## Community-Based Approaches

Engaging communities in risk assessment activities and raising awareness about flood management is crucial. This includes training on early warning systems for flooding and heavy rainfall events (Mukherjee et al., 2018).

## Policy and Planning

Incorporating climate change adaptation into development planning is critical. This includes enhancing urban resilience and managing land use to prevent deforestation, which exacerbates the effects of droughts (Bianchi & Kirk, 2011).

## Water Resource Management

Implementing sustainable groundwater management practices, including the construction of small-scale water storage bodies, promoting efficient irrigation techniques, and enhancing groundwater recharge can increase water availability during drought periods (Bianchi & Kirk, 2011).

#### Community-based adaptation strategies, including capacity building for local communities, are essential. This includes drought risk assessment and the design and implementation of disaster risk reduction measures that enhance local resilience (Munasinghe & Shaw, 2006).

##### Policy and Government Actions: Policies should support sustainable water management, promote water conservation, and provide incentives to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices. - **Saltwater inundation and intrusion and salinization of groundwater impacting coastal agricultural land productivity**

##### Infrastructure Enhancement and Protection: Strengthening coastal defenses such as sea walls, levees, and storm barriers. This is particularly important in low-lying areas that are exacerbated by rising sea levels. This also includes elevating roads and buildings to protect against flooding (Nepal et al., 2013).

###### Ecosystem-based Adaptation: Preserving and restoring mangroves, coral reefs, and other natural buffers that absorb the impacts of coastal erosion and storm surges. These ecosystems not only protect the coast but also provide habitat for biodiversity and fisheries.

##### Relocation and Retreat: Implementing planned relocation for communities living in areas at risk from sea-level rise. New settlements are built in safer areas. This strategy is often combined with other adaptation measures that may be necessary in highly vulnerable areas (Kimbles et al., 2023).

## Glacial lake outburst floods (GLOFs)
- Significant GLOF risk exists in some of the Himalayas, with projected increased hazard development as glaciers retreat and new lake forms.
- Some areas contain a potential reservoir for water in GLOF triggering processes, leading to three-fold increase in a different flood downstream areas in comparison (Li et al., 2019).
- In the eastern Indian Himalayas, hundreds of glacial lakes have been identified. However, certain lakes are classified as very high-risk due to various hazards such as potential avalanches and rockfalls.
- The 2014 GLOF in the Indian Himalayas highlighted the dynamic nature of these risks, with sudden glacier retreat leading to lake outburst and increased GLOFs. This calls for ongoing risk assessments and resilience planning for vulnerable communities (Wessels et al., 2016).

## Cyclones: climate change expected to increase
- Loss of Life and Property: Cyclones have historically caused substantial loss of life and property.

## Risk Assessment and Monitoring
- Extensive use of remote sensing and GIS technologies aids in monitoring glacial lakes and assessing their risk.
- Early warning systems are crucial for timely evacuation, enhancing community response and evacuation procedures.
- Community Engagement: Implementing early warning systems or better risk reduction strategies, including evacuation plans, is essential to enhance community response capabilities. This involves enhancing local capacities to respond to GLOFs.

## Artificial Lowering of Lake Levels
- Techniques such as siphoning or controlled drainage can reduce the water level in lakes deemed to be at high risk of causing GLOFs.
- The aim is to lower and then raise lake levels, thereby lowering the risk of sudden breaches (Kraftsone & Warzacki, 1995).

## Community-Based Disaster Preparedness
- Empowering local communities through awareness, training on rescue measures, and evacuation procedures is crucial. Enhancing local capacity is vital for effective disaster response and recovery (Dine et al., 2016).

## Frequency of the most extreme events
These storms result in damage to properties and destruction of food sources of life, primarily due to high winds, heavy rainfall, and storm surges (Jayakumar et al., 2015).

### Impact on Livelihoods:
Cyclones significantly impact agriculture productivity, especially in coastal regions. The associated heavy rain can lead to crop failures, loss of livestock, and damage to fishing infrastructure, which are critical sources of livelihood for many communities (Friedl et al., 2021).

## Economic Impacts
The aftermath of cyclones often causes a rise in poverty, disruption of communities, and long-term socio-economic dislocation. Recovery and resulting efforts can

### Infrastructure Resilience:
Strengthening buildings, communication systems, and transportation networks to withstand the force of cyclones by using cyclone-resistant structures and improving early warning systems to ensure timely evacuation (UNISDR, 2015).

### Integrated Water Resources Management:
Implementing water management strategies such as rainwater harvesting, controlled release of water from reservoirs, and preserving the integrity of rivers and wetlands (Palmerio et al., 2016).

## Ecosystem Protection
Protecting and restoring mangroves, coral reefs, and other natural buffers to mitigate the effects of cyclones. These ecosystems act as natural buffers against the force of cyclones.

### Policy and Governance:
Developing policies that prioritize disaster risk reduction in land use planning, investment in disaster risk reduction technologies, and public engagement in policy-making processes to ensure that adaptation programs are comprehensive and inclusive (Winchester, 2006).

#### Financial Implications of Climate Change

1. A report revealed that in 2022, India suffered a loss of 8% in GDP compared to a hypothetical world without climate change.
2. A latest report by The Reserve Bank of India predicts that up to 4.5 per cent of India's GDP could be at risk due to climate change.

#### The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) has focused on climate change as the primary theme in its Report on Currency and Finance (RCF) for 2022-23, a theme-based annual report on contemporary issues concerning central banking and macroeconomic policy. The RCF discusses how to meet climate goals as a policy priority and analyses the potential macro-financial consequences of climate change for India.

#### The theme for this report, published on May 1, 2023, is "Towards a Greener India Index," and it claims that fiscal policy has not been sufficiently supportive in addressing climate change. It also highlights the urgent need for a green taxonomy, a classification system that determines whether an investment is sustainable or not.

#### The report looks into the physical indicators of climate change and policy action globally and in India. It goes on to discuss the macroeconomic effect of climate change in India, climate change and the financial sector, and policy options to mitigate these effects. The report says that the Indian economy has been resilient against climate change, with a reduction in the energy intensity of GDP by around 5% annually and a significant improvement in its energy mix in favor of renewables. However, it also notes that India's carbon emissions have increased at an average rate of about 6% per annum till 2030," says the report, quantifying the scale of the effort needed towards climate action.

#### The financial stability of banks around the globe is gradually broadening their objectives beyond the original scope or focus, the report says. The pressure of currency depreciation in countries frequently affected by climate disasters can cause financial instability.
3. Giving other examples of how climate change can affect inflation and financial assets, the report says that climate change can affect price stability through supply shocks such as food and energy shortages.

#### Additionally, demand shocks can arise when firms and households lose wealth on account of frequent natural disasters. This can lead to a reduction in spending by individuals and businesses, limiting the flow of credit to banks.

## MSMEs in India

40. The MSME sector plays a pivotal role from various economic and social perspectives in India. The share of gross value added (GVA) from the MSME sector to India's GDP was 30.9% in 2018-19, and the share of exports was 65.9% in 2018-19.

41. **Definition and Categorization of MSMEs in India:**

| Enterprise Category | Criteria based on investment in plant & machinery / Equipment or turnover |
|---|---|
| Micro | Investment up to INR 1 crore (=USD 125,000) and turnover up to INR 5 crore (=USD 625,000) |
| Small | Investment up to INR 10 crore (=USD 1.25 million) and turnover up to INR 50 crores (=USD 5.25 million) |
| Medium | Investment up to INR 50 crores (=USD 6.25 million) and turnover up to INR 250 crores (=USD 25.25 million) |

42. The MSME sector in India comprises of around 33.88 million units engaged in either manufacturing or services or trading activities, creating employment for about 110 million people, contributing about 45% to manufacturing output and about 40% of the total industrial production. About 67% of MSMEs are involved in manufacturing of goods such as textiles, leather products, ranging from traditional to high-tech, that are manufactured by Indian MSMEs today. The sector is the biggest employer in India with a significant contribution towards employment and economic growth.

The table below shows the trend analysis of MSMEs based on their technology profile. The sector consumes about 25% of the total energy consumed by the industrial sector in India. Trend analysis indicates that the MSME sector has a high potential for energy conservation, both through improved energy consumption including both thermal and electrical energy consumption. The projected energy consumption shows a steep increase from 1990 to 2030.

The barriers in MSME sector towards adopting climate-smart technologies is also highlighted by the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) submitted by Government of India to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

**MSME role in adoption of adaptation technologies**

50. Adaptation actions have enormous potential for investment from private sector tapping through potential business opportunities. Although MSMEs are themselves vulnerable to climate change, they still have important roles to play in helping communities adapt and build resilience against the impacts of climate change on their most vulnerable populations. MSMEs are closely integrated into their communities, and as a result, have local knowledge of conditions that can be used for adaptation planning. They also have access to a wide range of climate-related information. This may be especially useful for adoption, since climate change impacts vary depending on where places sit within the country or region. MSMEs are also well positioned to provide a wide range of climate-resilient products. In general, small and medium firms don’t have the same governance structure as larger corporations, allowing them to be more flexible in their response to changing conditions. They can also be more nimble in helping communities members to build adaptive practices. All of these actions on can go a long way toward helping communities prepare for current and future climate change impacts.

78 percent of the population, are in a better position to get products and services to hard-to-reach populations and communities. MSMEs also have an important role to play in providing access to clean water, sanitation, and positioning to develop customized and innovative products and services in areas of agriculture and livestock sector including climate-resilient seeds, drought-resistant crops, improved irrigation systems, rainwater harvesting, clean and affordable drinking water, water harvesting, recycling and Municipal solid waste management, etc. However, start-up costs for MSMEs are often high and they may not have the resources to invest in new technologies or innovative business models require customized and dedicated financial support to meet their investment needs, which hinders their ability to adapt.

**Adaptation Needs**

51. The Climate Policy Initiative highlights the following barriers to adaptation financing in India (a) Limited knowledge of investment opportunities; (b) Lack of capacity to develop commercially feasible projects; (c) Unavailability of bankable project proposals; and (d) Inability to access finance. These are also reflected by the findings from the survey, and in most of India’s technology/technology providers are usually start-ups that comes under MSMEs. Hence, the need is to create a conducive environment for adaptation financing and to provide adequate support to finance in selected MSMEs. The survey further focuses on regions in India with high climatic vulnerabilities: coastal belt and Indian Himalayan regions and the focus sectors would be agriculture, water resources, agroforestry, disaster resilience.

52. The potential impacts of climate change on businesses can be classified as either direct or indirect. Direct impacts of climate change are those that occur directly from changes in weather patterns such as increased frequency of extreme infrastructure and indirect impacts of climate change affect business operations through changes in the business environment. These include changes to the availability of raw materials, energy costs, and access to markets and effects on stakeholders. Companies that are not prepared to manage the direct and indirect impacts of climate may face significant financial losses or even go out of business as a result of these adverse climate change related impacts. This is even more true for India’s MSMEs. For most of them it has a higher priority to manage immediate challenges such as access to finance, raw materials, energy costs, etc., and they are not prepared to manage the risks that financial resources to endure even a single rupture in supply or demand, caused for instance by an extreme weather event or a new regulation on climate change.

**Impact of climate change on businesses**

* Rising temperatures: Average temperatures as well as temperature extremes have been increasing and projected to further increase across India. For business, temperature rises are likely to have an indirect impact such as increased energy costs, higher water consumption, and more frequent maintenance required in manufacturing processes. Rising temperatures can also negatively affect crop yield and harvests.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 24

## Precipitation changes: Higher precipitation in some regions, and less rainfall in others have direct impacts on yields, building, sewage and ground water availability.

### Loss of freshwater: A report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) states that water and air may become scarce for millions of people and causes for issues for societies and the environment under climate change. The industry demand for water in India is estimated to more than triple between 2000 and 2025. Droughts are also expected to increase, leading to a greater need for irrigation systems and higher costs for the cost and productivity of companies. Furthermore, water being typically one of the most contentious issues with respect to climate change, it is likely that conflicts over water will increase.

### Price of energy: Climate change affects both supply and demand of electricity; while higher temperatures lead to an increased demand for energy, e.g., for air conditioning and cooling, it also affects supply, e.g. by decreasing the output from hydroelectric power plants. The industry demand for electricity in India is estimated to triple between 2006 and 2025. For all these reasons, energy prices are likely to show greater volatility and unpredictability.

### Regulatory requirements: Both the need to reduce greenhouse gas emissions for mitigating climate change and the need to adapt to the impacts of climate change have already triggered policy action, changing the sustainability agenda in India. The regulatory framework is evolving rapidly, especially in the fields of emission reduction and resource consumption. Besides, national and state level policies are relevant not only for Indian businesses, exporting MSMEs need to also anticipate the policies of foreign countries.

## Leveraging Private Sector Investment for financing MSMEs

### 53. India needs substantial amounts of climate finance by 2050 to achieve its ambitious sustainability goals. An estimated $1 trillion is needed in order to meet the country’s adaptation and mitigation targets, so financing that can bring in private sector investment is vital to scaling climate solutions. However, most financial institutions are not yet prepared for this challenge. According to a survey by Financial Corporates (NFC) Ltd it does not prominently incorporate the financing landscape for climate technologies into their stated policy or strategy. The results of the survey also indicate that only 10% of banks and other financial institutions (BFIs) adopt a “cautious but watch” stance, refraining from explicit commitments to climate financing. As a result, at the recent G20 Leaders’ Summit in Osaka, Japan, India’s Prime Minister Narendra Modi announced the launch of a $5 billion Green Climate Fund (GCF) for private sector investment.

### 54. The findings of the survey by RBI vis-a-vis notification dated July 27, 2012 and also as indicated in RBI’s Financial Sector Stability Report (FSSR), 2013, indicate that while there is a growing awareness among banks and other financial institutions towards climate financing, are presently quite low and there is a need for training, sensitization and capacity building of the banking sector. The survey also indicates that there is a need to develop and integrate suitable frameworks for MSMEs ESG & gender, etc. Further, RBI vis-a-vis draft disclosure framework on climate related financial risks, 2014 vide its notification dated February 28, 2014 has indicated the need to develop a comprehensive framework for integrating climate risk management in banks and other financial institutions.

### 55. Moreover, in the recent visit of G20 Delegations, the leaders unanimously acknowledged that there is an urgent need to scale up climate finance for MSMEs. The private sector can play a key role in scaling up climate finance for MSMEs. Further many of these FPIs have better outreach, especially in rural areas, thus they are more likely to be used by small and petty traders who are able to benefit from the current intervention.

### 54. The objective of the programme is to develop and demonstrate an ecosystem to promote Climate Financing to MSMEs in Africa, through a combination of grants and concessional loans. This will be achieved by mobilizing up to US$10 million in reimbursable funds (grant) or up to US$15 million for technical assistance, and the use of the reimbursable funds (loan) to provide concessional loans to MSMEs. The total amount of funding available is US$25 million, with a maximum loan size of US$800 thousand. Out of total financing, SDBI will be on-lent (i.e.) USD 100 million directly to MSMEs; and the USD 15 million technical assistance will be used for capacity building and awareness raising activities. The private sector investment workstream has been allocated US$2400 thousand. The concessional funds are expected to trigger the market towards Green Climate Fund (GCF) investments.

The programme’s programme's activities have been structured as two components, which collectively contribute to the achievement of three outcomes, with corresponding outputs and activities presented below:

| Component | Output | Activity |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Component 1 - Term loans to support climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs. | Output 1.1: Enable adoption technologies financed and implemented by SDBI to scale up the use of these technologies for mitigation finance for MSMEs | Activity 1.1.1: Establish an loan instrument to provide concessional loans for MSME mitigation needs |
| Component 2 - Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs through a dedicated fund. | Output 1.2: Enable adoption technologies financed and implemented by SDBI to scale up the use of these technologies for adaptation finance for MSMEs | Activity 1.2.1: Establish a loan instrument to provide concessional loans for MSME adaptation |
| Component 2 - Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs through a dedicated fund. | Output 2.1: Capacity built and improved information systems to support climate change adaptation and mitigation finance for MSMEs & PFIs; Capabilities of SDBI staff enhanced to rapidly scale their business and climate impact | Activity 2.1.1: Undertake baseline studies for 500 MSMEs |
| Component 2 - Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs through a dedicated fund. | Output 2.2: Enabling environment for the green climate-resilient MSME sector | Activity 2.2.1: Develop and implement MSME climate adaptation and resilience benchmarking tools |
| Component 2 - Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs through a dedicated fund. | Output 2.2: Enabling environment for the green climate-resilient MSME sector | Activity 2.2.2: Digital Green Credit Rating Tool (CCF) development and deployment of Green parameters (Energy and Environment) with Credit rating tool |

### Key barriers and challenges

Despite the urgent importance and scope for low carbon transition of sector MSMEs have limited financial capacity to base investments in climate-resilient technologies. The current incentives and financing schemes are limited from the perspective of MSMEs especially given the investment requirements for climate-resilient technologies which can be as high as 10-25% more than conventional technologies. The average credit line offered by banks is estimated at USD 240 billion (about $8,66 billion as of 2018 estimates). In addition to inadequate access to finance and lack of technical expertise, MSMEs are also constrained by the limited financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient technologies. Additionally, MSMEs often lack sufficient collateral for accessing loans.

**Barrier 1:** Limited operating in climate-critical sectors risks investments due to high operational costs, limited financial capacity, potentially volatile conditions, and additional lack of sufficient collateral.

* **Impact of the barrier:** This leads to a reluctance to pursue financing or offering investment opportunities with higher interest rates and shorter repayment periods. Lack of collateral further hinders MSMEs from accessing necessary funding.
* **Project intervention:** Provision of concessional loans to make climate-resilient projects viable for financial institutions

**Barrier 2:**

* **Impact of the barrier:** This leads to a reluctance to pursue financing or offering investment opportunities with higher interest rates and shorter repayment periods. Lack of collateral further hinders MSMEs from accessing necessary funding.
* **Project intervention:** Provision of concessional loans to make climate-resilient projects viable for financial institutions

## Barrier: Lack of capacity and awareness among PFIs are a barrier to lending to MSMEs in order climate-homogeneous regions. They will have limited lenders providing them required finance for the transition towards climate-resilient and low-emitting projects, especially those MSMEs located in climatic vulnerable regions.

This barrier will be overcome by top of mindering and building the capacity of PFIs towards understanding climate change and its impact on MSMEs through a dedicated climate focused ground vertical. Further, SIDBI through its direct lending to MSMEs for implementing energy efficient technologies will help identify the risks associated with climate change and provide technical assistance to Indian banks/IFIs to formulate/customize financing schemes accordingly.

## Barrier: Capital and access to finance markets are also barriers to supply of funding by PFIs to MSMEs.

The programme will help in complementing the efforts of the Reserve Bank of India (RBI) in putting out place suitable systems and processes for providing climate change related credit in the NEFCO/FFC which would add to the existing financial instruments. Further, SIDBI through its direct lending to MSMEs for implementing energy efficient technologies will help showcase best practices to evaluate the risks associated with climate change and also enable a series of innovative financing schemes for both green and risk and direct equity investments.

## Barrier: Knowledge and capacity barriers for the expansion of low emission resilient products and MSE value chains.

There is a growing consensus in various multilateral forums that climate change will have significant impact on climate finance. Lack of understanding and capacity among MSMEs to adopt and implement climate technologies is one of the most important challenges faced by the MSME sector in India. The current incentive schemes do not

## Barrier: Knowledge and capacity barriers for the expansion of low emission resilient products and MSE value chains.

Due to the inherent small size and limited capacities, the MSMEs promoted in a window looking after many day-to-day activities like production, inventory management, collection of receivables, etc. hence, the MSMEs are not able to invest in climate technologies. It is one of the most important challenges faced by the MSME sector in India. The current incentive schemes do not

This barrier will be overcome by the development of sectoral adaptive and innovative financing schemes for MSMEs contributions to policy initiatives for Climate Change Mitigation and adaptation. SIDBI will provide recommendations for an energy savings trading scheme for the MSME sector.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 28

## Environment Policy Barriers

- Provide enough motivation to the borrowers for adoption / enhancement of their performance with respect to the environment.
- The existing policies and regulations in India prescribed the minimum performance standards for energy efficiency, which are not sufficient as incentives to improve their performance on a continual basis. This is further compounded by the lack of supporting motivation to the Indian MSMEs such as trading of energy credits, etc., which also an enabler and enabler requires a policy change.
- It has been observed that generally the modern / latest technologies are not available in India for adoption of original or fabricated technologies. Further, MSMEs also face high upfront costs associated with the adoption of new technologies and recover its investment from savings at a much later stage which is usually after 3 to 5 years of designs etc. Usually, such proposals take longer time, which is beyond the horizon of most financial institutions and also delayed decisions of MSMEs to go for such investments.
- MSMEs are slow to adopt new technologies and continue to use the outdated technologies which may be highly inefficient in terms of energy consumption. This is a need to build local sectoral expertise through feasibility studies, technical assistance, training programs and knowledge transfers both in public and private sector. MSMEs also lack the required skills and experience for operations.
- Not many replied / large-scale Original Equipment Manufacturers (OEM) are willing to enter this sector as a potential customer for certain reasons including high upfront costs, low volumes, etc., and the nature of local channel partners. Exposure of MSMEs towards available technologies is limited due to which awareness is often limited and depends on either the Industrial Association or technology or system integrators already available in the local market.

### Barriers

#### Solar - Open Access
- Lack of partnership opportunities for large developers with MSMEs.
- Low capital investment opportunity, especially high upfront costs preventing large developers to partner with MSMEs.
- Limited awareness among MSMEs about the risky nature of the segment; financing is only limited to small-medium sized businesses with large requirements (NAB-10-20 C).
- Micro enterprises are not able to leverage such financing.

#### Roof Top Solar
- Limited awareness (>50% MSMEs across clusters weren't aware of RTS) and lack of awareness about the benefits.
- Financing Challenge (Limited ability to put capital upfront)
- Lack of awareness among MSMEs between low officer rating and challenges in collection at disaggregated level

#### Energy Efficiency
- Inherent inability to access capex easily limits the degree to which they can adopt these interventions within their operations

**Page 33**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 29

## Electric Vehicle

- **Electric buses and trucks:** In the case of electric buses and trucks, the initial cost is about two to three times higher than that of diesel counterparts.
- **Access to finance:** At present, the financing terms are not viable for most of the electric vehicles. Banks with better financing terms in form of high loan-to-value ratio (LTV) are reluctant to provide loans due to perceived risks. The low LTV is translating into higher upfront capital costs and longer payback periods.
- **Technical:** At present, there are few products available in the market for electric vehicles in the two-wheeler segment and three-wheeler cargo segment but are missing from the other segments such as passenger cars, buses, school buses, employee transport, intracity, intercity etc. and requires models of electric vehicles that can cater to these segments.
- **Regulatory:** Currently, only a few countries have policies available at the central government level only with a limited number of vehicles. The demand for electric vehicles is not in line with its clear vision towards transition to electric vehicles.

## Adaptation

### Lack Limited Bankability
- **Narrative gap:** Information gaps (i.e., poor data and analytics) around climate risks and the adaptation response.
- **Action is weak:** "Cost of reaction" not well quantified, existing solutions are ad-hoc and mono-sectoral.

### Project Structure & Complexity


---
**Page 34**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 12.0 | PAGE 30

* Projects cut across traditional sectors, require multi-stakeholder alignment (govts., communities, funders, etc.), increasing complexity
* The need for innovative financial instruments to mobilize risk-return impact expectations to come together, increasing structuring complexity
* Consultations with leading global DFIs has revealed a two-fold challenge in disbursing climate finance
    * There is a need for credible intermediaries who can act as a bridge between the public and private sectors
    * The lack of innovative financial instruments to mobilize risk-return impact expectations, which are critical to scale up large pipeline of investible projects and help unlock 4x-10x private investments will be critical
* The biggest challenge to mobilize climate finance is not the availability of capital but the lack of innovative financial instruments

## Barriers to climate finance for India's MSEMs

60. India needs substantial amounts of concessional finance by 2030 to achieve its ambitious sustainability goals. An estimated $1 trillion will be required to be mobilized by 2030. Public investment alone was insufficient to tackle India’s adaptation and mitigation targets, so financing that can bring in private sector investment is vital to scaling climate solutions across the country.
61. However, the National Green Bonds Framework (NGBF) and National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development Companies (NBFCs) do not prominently incorporate the financing landscape for climate technologies into stated objectives. The NGBF has been criticized for its lack of ambition in terms of scale and scope, while NBFCs have adopted a cautious “wait and watch” stance, refraining from explicit commitments to climate financing.
62. As per the findings of the survey on RBI vet its notification dated July 27, 2022 and also as indicated in RBI’s Report on Currency and Finance 2022-23 issued on May 03, 2023, it is observed that the financing flows from the banking sector towards MSEMs are still very low. The survey also reveals that there has been a lack of awareness among MSEMs about the banking reforms in this regard as also putting into place proper systems and processes towards climate risk assessments and internal rating.
63. Consultations with the wider MSEM ecosystem comprising of leading financial institutions (including SBI) on ground realities have revealed that the primary financing need for MSEMs is to provide timely financial support, customized financial products, and services, holding workloads on climate resilience for MSEMs, etc.

## Financial Institutions

* Financial institutions felt that they will be comfortable to provide climate financing for the MSEMs sector only if they get low-cost funding from the government. They also highlighted that there should be a dedicated fund or window to support MSEMs in developing climate finance products, forbearance in NPA classification of climate finance loans, blended funding, longer tenure loans, detailed guarantees system knowledge of microfinance institutions, etc.
* To enhance their lending capacity, financial institutions should offer subsidies, especially in the area of bringing out low-cost SME-focused financial products
* FIs also suggested that all types of climate financing be included in priority sector and inclusion of climate finance in risk assessment

## Future

In future if financial institutions are mandated to disclose their emissions, including Scope 3 emissions, this could result in some difficulties in obtaining finance, which could be overcome with better preparations and efforts on the part of MSEMs in curbing emissions.

More studies need to be done for more training, building greater awareness for MSEMs on how they can see the bigger picture and contribute to avoid adverse climate change - very few MSEMs were found to know what the impact of their actions on climate is and they need to be made aware of how to manufacture their products more responsibly and enhance their resilience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 32

## B

The primary goal of the project will be to alleviate the financing barriers by reducing out-of-pocket costs. This will prompt operators to start electrification as government subsidies are withdrawn. The increase in uptake of electric vehicles (EVs) will lead to increased demand for charging stations due to increased demand. Thus, by addressing the technology and structural barriers, the programme shall contribute towards the achievement of the targets set at MSME customers, especially fleet operators.

Furthermore, the project will also aim to address the gaps at a regulatory level which will be used to formulate relevant policy solutions. The policy solutions developed in this regard will be designed on the basis of project outcomes and will be driven to increase availability of products (old and new), and reduction in battery prices.

The support in adaptation field for MSMEs is one of the key focus areas of the project.

## Theory of Change diagram

64. Based on SIDBI's experience and learnings from financing MSMEs in a, a detailed theory of change has been devised to address the opportunities and challenges mentioned above while catalysing financing for climate technologies directly as well indirectly through PFIs.


| Barrier | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Lack of awareness | The lack of awareness among MSMEs regarding the benefits of electrification and the availability of financial products. |
| Lack of capacity | The lack of technical expertise, skills, and knowledge required to implement electrification projects. |
| High cost of financing | The high cost of financing for electrification projects due to limited access to affordable finance options. |
| Regulatory barriers | The regulatory hurdles that MSMEs face in implementing electrification projects, such as the need for permits and approvals from various authorities. |

## Project activities

### Output
- Increased adoption of electric vehicles (EVs) among MSMEs.
- Expansion of charging infrastructure for EVs.

### Barriers
- Lack of awareness about the benefits of electrification.
- Limited technical expertise and skills.
- High cost of financing.
- Regulatory hurdles.

### Assumptions
- The project will increase awareness among MSMEs regarding the benefits of electrification.
- The project will provide training to MSMEs on how to implement electrification projects.
- The project will secure funding for electrification projects through PFIs and other financial mechanisms.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V 12.0 | PAGE 34

## Programme Description

65. SDI through the programme Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Project in MSMEs aims to develop and demonstrate an ecosystem to promote Climate Financing to MSMEs in mitigation and adaptation interventions. To achieve this GCF will provide USD 8 million as a grant component, which will be blended with co-finance from SIDBI of USD 800 million to finance the MSMEs for their green interventions. Out of the total amount of USD 160 million, USD 24 million will be used in the form of grants and USD 136 million as various PFIs as on-lending/financings. To achieve results at a scale, private sector investment worth USD 2400 million is required to be mobilized.

66. The proposed activities in the programme emanates from SIDBI's more than a decade experience of ecosystem strengthening aligned to climate actions for MSMEs. Based on our experience, the following elements will be considered as part of the programme:

### a. Handholding: Besides financing, MSMEs require technical handholding support in implementing climate change mitigation and adaptation interventions.

### b. Market Creation: There needs to be a market pull for the MSMEs to adopt climate technologies. Since, MSMEs is how India's economy is structured, it is important that they are able to access finance from various programmes, which will help in demonstrating the climate technologies that MSMEs can adopt within their clusters.

### c. Capacity Building: It was seen that training and capacity building of MSMEs, developing e-modules of the training content, and creating awareness among not just MSMEs but also the financial institutions. Additionally, stakeholders advocated for more training, building greater awareness for MSMEs on how they can see the bigger picture better, and contribute to overall adaptation efforts.

### d. Ease of Access: Simplified end-to-end process would boost demand from MSMEs to demand for climate financing. The simplified loan application process has proven to be very helpful in the expansion of the programme on the whole.

### e. Mainstreaming Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation: In India, the banking system, more focused on MSMEs. Currently, financing green technologies to MSMEs, especially those related to energy efficiency, is still a nascent area. Hence, there is an urgent need for banks to understand the climate space as also to enhance their lending portfolios focused towards climate financing to MSMEs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V12.0 | PAGE 35

## Refinancing of the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-financing, to the PFIs.
The Programme will offer refinancing against eligible assets created by the enhanced PFIs and originated on longer than six months prior to approval of the proposal. The PFIs are defined as commercial banks (including SBI), DFIs, NBFIs under the institutional finance mandate, SIDBI's continued participation with Indian Banking System through its credit line, and NBFCs under the institutional finance mandate. SIDBI's continued participation with Indian Banking System through its credit line.

### Refinance to banks
SIDBI expands resources of other banks/PFIs through refinancing activities thereby flowing the cost of credit to the end borrowers.
- Portfolio: As on FY 2023, the portfolio was Rs.95,841 crore (NBFIs) and Rs.6,173 crores; the year-on-year growth was a significant 7%.
- The portfolio is in respect of 40 banks which comprises of 12 Public Sector Banks (PSBs), 18 Private Sector Banks (PSBs), 5 State Bank of India (SBI) branches, 10 Small Finance Banks (SFIs), Regional Rural Banks (RRBs), and 1 Urban Cooperative Bank (UCB).

### Refinance to Non-Banking Financial Companies (NBFCs)
- SIDBI refinances the NBFIs through its credit line.
- Portfolio: As on FY 2023, the portfolio stood at Rs.498.6 crore (NBFIs) and Rs.13.41 crores across 56 NBFCs; an increase of 66% in outstanding. During FY 2023, sanctions and disbursements showed a growth by 67% and 81%, respectively.
- Refinance to Micro Finance Institutions (MFIs):
- SIDBI extended financial support to MFIs in the form of equity/quality-ease-refinace, line term, grant support, etc., enabling them to scale up their lending portfolio towards climate finance.
- Portfolio: As on FY 2023, the portfolio stood at Rs.596.38 crore (NBFIs) and Rs.1.490 crores as of FY 2023 against as USD 375.56 million (NBFIs); a 31.4% crores during FY 2022, registering growth of 37%, over the previous year.
- NBFCs emerging interest towards green/economic financing:
- The areas of interest for NBFCs in the consultation were as follows: the highlights are:
    * 17 NBFCs participated in the consultation
    * 90% of them agreed that Green Finance represents a significant opportunity for their business.
- The priority areas overwhelmingly emerged as (a) Electric Mobility (3W and EVs) (b). Solar Roofs (residential and commercial)

### Indian FI Interest in climate financing
The interest shown by the Indian FIs and NBFCs regarding climate financing. Hence, there is a need to sensitize the PFIs to increase their lending portfolio towards climate financing of MSMEs.
- Concessional loans to PFIs for further action as a booster to this objective.
- The programme will also build synergy and leverage from the experiences of pilot projects launched by SIDBI in collaboration with various stakeholders, including the successful scaling up of some of the earlier initiatives, taken by God of India and SIDBI towards creating momentum in climate financing. These include:
    * Green Bonds
    * Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP)
    * Green Infrastructure Fund (GIF)
- Initiatives including successful implementation of GEF funded World Bank project: "Focusing Energy Efficiency at MSMEs in India" under the umbrella of the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and Japan International Co-operation Agency (JICA).
- Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), Agence Francaise de Developpement (AFD), etc.

### A joint approach of Mitigation and Adaptation is more effective than the focus only on energy efficiency.
The success of climate/energy financing project, particularly for MSMEs require both technical handholding support and financial support. The PFIs can be sensitized to increase their lending portfolio towards climate finance by appointing climate climate MSME ambassadors and pool of champions until they are a key investment decision by majority of MSMEs as they follow the 'Neighbourhood Theory'.

### Neighbourhood theory refers to the phenomena wherein new technologies or interventions are adopted in a particular region, often based on the larger industry landscape based on showcasing of individual success stories.
This holds particularly true for the Indian MSME sectors where most constituents are related first movers. In this regard, SIDBI has been successful in scaling up its initiatives through the 'Neighbourhood Theory' mechanism followed by a real world on ground showcases of the technology/intervention's benefits is seen to catalyse the unit's neighbour into adopting the same technology/intervention.

#### Under consideration: ~10000 number of loans will be provided to MSMEs as detailed below:

- **Financial Instruments**: PFIs may use their existing financial instruments or design and implement climate lending products within their asset portfolio, in line with the green financial instruments of SDBI.
- **SDBI Selection Criteria**: SDBI will select the PFIs based on their asset portfolio and experience.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V12.0 | PAGE 37

### B

**Programme Outputs and Activities**

#### Output 1.1: MSEME adaptation technologies financed and implemented. Increased access to mitigation finance for MSEMEs.

##### Activity 1.1.1: Establish a loan instrument to provide concessional loans for MSEME mitigation needs.

74. The programme intends to offer concessional loans for investments aimed at financing selected MSEMEs. The key intervention of technologies to be financed under FMAP are (a) renewable Energy PV systems Modest Captive project, up to capacity of 100kWp; (b) energy efficiency in industrial processes and appliances, insulation, high performance glass, LED lighting, High-Efficiency Heat Pumps, solar water heater etc. SIDBs has identified a list of MSEMEs that are interested in adopting these technologies.

The same SOPs would be followed while assessing an intended borrower i.e., MSEME for providing debt financing. A detailed technical and financial assessment will be carried out to determine the loan amount and repayment terms.

75. The programme has been designed to offer concessional loans to PFIs (NBFIs/Capital FMs) and driving them to further lend to MSEMEs. This is in line with the objective of the Green Climate Fund (GCF). To achieve this, the programme intends to encourage the PFIs to create an ecosystem of climate financing which would allow them to provide concessional loans to MSEMEs. The PFIs will be encouraged to adopt a risk-sharing mechanism with SIDBs directly lending to the MSEMEs as an example for the PFIs to follow so successfully lend to the MSEMEs for adopting low-carbon and resilient technologies.

##### Other TA elements include:
- Carry out detailed technical assessment of potential MSEME units.
- Carry out credit risk rating evaluation of potential MSEME units.
- Sensitization of MSEMEs on climate finance, based on the technical and risk assessment results.
- Incentivize the units to create climate MSEME ambassadors and a group of champion units.
- Drive up take-up of climate technologies by MSEMEs.
- End to end technical and handsholding support to MSEMs, PFIs, NBFIs, and other stakeholders.
- Facilitate networking of technology providers.

##### Activity 1.1.1 and 1.2.1 will jointly address the barriers related to:
- Lack of private investment at the MSEME level due to sustainability concerns, high capital costs for climate products, and a limited understanding of MSEME sector by institutional financial players.
This will be achieved by (a) Offering concessional finance to MSEMEs directly encouraging them to adopt climate technologies; (b) Providing technical assistance to MSEMEs in accessing capital and blended financial products from PFIs which will help in reducing their risk of lending towards MSEMs thus encouraging PFIs to lend to MSEMEs. This will also reduce the cost of capital for MSEMEs, thereby reducing risks of financing MSEMs and developing the requisite underlying skills to finance climate technologies within the MSEME sector; (c) Providing technical assistance to MSEMEs in accessing blended financial products which will address the need for banking off-facilitators, enhancing climate risk assessment processes, and integrating MGGD, ESG, and green lending practices into PFIs’ business models. This will also help in developing a conducive climate financing, aiming to encourage other financial institutions to also engage in climate finance for MSEMs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 38

B

## Observations

The large number of providers of such adaptive technologies/interventions are MSEMs. Hence, the objective of this activity is to create a debt instrument tailored for improved access to finance in select MSEMs that offer adaptive technologies and interventions. The proposed debt instrument will be structured as a bond with an interest rate of 5 per cent. The programme would focus on regions in India with high climate vulnerability: coastal, east and Himalayan regions and the focus sectors would be agriculture, water resources, forestry, disasters and services. An indicative list of relevant technologies is provided below.

### Adaptation Interventions

The adaptation intervention under this programme aims to tackle both the demand and supply side concerns. On the demand side, there are limited financial resources available for MSEMs to invest in climate-resilient infrastructure. The proposed debt instrument will provide a mechanism through which these MSEMs have very limited knowledge of technological solutions to make them climate resilient.

On the supply side, there is a lack of awareness among MSEMs about the availability of credit from banks and other suppliers to larger enterprises manufacturing technologies related to adaptation technology. Hence, focus will also be on financing MSEMs for their capacity building in order to enable them to access these technologies.

### Adaptation Projects

Adaptation projects are considered to have limited bankability due to limited financial returns, lack of synergies with existing projects and the need for a longer time horizon. The proposed debt instrument is expected to provide an alternative source of financing for MSEMs that offer relevant adaptive technologies and services to wider adaptation projects.

In order to ensure the viability of concessional debt, SBI intends to bring in expertise of different financial instruments for adaptation new.

### Indicative List of Adaptation Solutions

The following are indicative list of adaptation solutions as detailed below. The adaptation technologies are ever evolving especially quick response to emerging climate induced hazards and alternative measures/technologies will be considered they are deemed fit going forward:

| S.N | Adaptive Measure | Sectors |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | More effective water treatment facilities and a range of other water related relief efforts, including recycling and technologies to reduce leakages | Water |
| 2 | Various measures for building resilience in housing, roofing and insulation | Disaster resilience |
| 3 | Software applications on mobile devices concerning climate related services | Disaster Response |
| 4 | New construction materials, technologies, and processes as well as new approach to design and planning | Disaster resilience |
| 5 | Climate-related insurance offerings, from building damage to crop losses (new products) | Disaster response |
| 6 | Other financial products from simple loans to help三农 pay for adaptation work, to climate derivatives and investment funds | Agriculture |
| 7 | Risk management services | Agriculture/Disaster Response |
| 8 | Weather and other information services | Agriculture/Disaster response |
| 9 | Supplying alternative raw materials for those that are at risk from climate change | Disaster Response |

### Indicative Technologies

These indicative technologies are relevant across regions in India as showcased in the table below:

| Region | Hazards | Risk/Impact | Adaptation Interventions | Technological Solutions |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Coastal Belt | Cyclones, sea level rise, extreme weather events, droughts, coastal floods | More than 60 per cent of coastal districts are vulnerable to extreme event hotspots | Weather forecasting and early warning systems to identify the climate related |
