#### Component 2 — Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs

**Output 2.1:** Capacity build and institutional management improved to support climate change adaptation and mitigation measures and PIs. Capacity of MSMEs, FPIs, and PFIs is built up to quickly scale their businesses and climate impact.

84. In addition to providing concessional finances, a range of TA activities will be carried out. As with the case within mitigation, these activities will cover ensuring the health of MSMEs’ viability of product in question and assisting the wider F1 network in financing through these interventions:

| Component 2 — Technical support for climate change adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs |
| --- |
| Output 2.1: Capacity build and institutional management improved to support climate change adaptation and mitigation measures and PIs. Capacity of MSMEs, FPIs, and PFIs is built up to quickly scale their businesses and climate impact. |

85. The programme will extend technical assistance (TA) to MSMEs and PFIs in order to help them gain access to low-carbon and climate resilient technologies. MSMEs will also be facilitated to obtain bankable business proposals and financial instruments from the Fund of the project.

86. The programme will strengthen the governance of other financial institutions as a continuous process adopted by SIDBI under various programmes such as PSRF where 15 banks/PFIs/FCIs are empowered with SIDBI to support financing through pre-approval letters. This will be further extended in the proposed programme to include PFIs and FPIs. The programme will also aim at building capacity of PFIs and FPIs to support them in enhancing their portfolios and to strengthen their capacities to appraise loans for MSMEs, focused on climate change adaptation and mitigation.

87. The programme will also focus on the development of a knowledge base on low-carbon and climate resilient technologies through a series of workshops and training sessions. This will be further extended beyond the project period in order to support programmes supporting the wider F1 ecosystem in line to understand MSME climate finance landscape which is a critical part of the TA activities in this programme. Successful implementation of these activities is expected to play a key role towards achieving the overall objective.

87a. The best practices and learning of the programmes will be shared with larger audiences including other MSMEs through e-newsletters, workshops, seminars, etc., which will be developed by SIDBI under the proposed programme and will be established in the proposed program in digital form which will be maintained by SIDBI beyond project period and will be available for public access.

88. The proposed programme has been designed incorporating and building on learnings acquired by SIDBI over time (as showcased in preceding sections). Further, the program will build on the existing knowledge landscape developed by SIDBI under its ongoing schemes.

89. The necessity of these activities has been determined through multiple stakeholder conversations with players across the value chain including MSMEs, technology developers, PFIs, local communities etc. Based their inputs, we have considered numerous TA related activities that have been included in our proposal which are designed to address climate concerns in the long-term. The activities under this component will be carried out by engaging technical support stakeholders.

**Activity 2.1.1:** Undertake baseline studies for MSMEs (500 units)

90. MSMEs have limited knowledge and understanding of the low-carbon and climate resilient technologies and need to be provided with a clear picture on how these technologies in terms of energy saving, GHG emission reduction, enhanced operational efficiency, long-term monitor benefits, etc. These technologies need assessments will be undertaken by:

- Identify the baseline parameters and current consumption of energy, water, etc.
- Measure the baseline emissions
- Demonstrate the savings possibilities in terms of specific energy consumption, GHG emission reductions, cost savings, etc.

### Section 92: Technology Assessment Studies

The technology assessment studies include feasibility and technical studies of specific technologies identified for adoption in a particular MSME, energy audits of MSME units, lifecycle analysis of products or services that help the MSMEs to assess their environmental impact, and awareness raising on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures and their benefits. These baseline studies will help in building awareness on the climate technologies and create niche markets for these technologies.

#### Activities 92.1-92.3

* **These assessments will be held on SIBDI’s previous work on sectoral technology gap mapping of MSMEs units.**
* The technology need assessment is imperative for those MSMEs in regions of high climate vulnerability prone to climate change impacts and who are not aware about the available climate technologies or the specific technologies that can make their respective units resilient.
* Specific technologies financed under this programme will help the MSMEs to equip themselves against the climatic hazards that they are most vulnerable.

### Section 94: Activity 2.1.2: Awareness raising and training on climate change adaptation and mitigation for MSMEs and Financial Institutions

This activity addresses the skills and capacity gaps in MSMEs, financial institutions in adapting climate technologies, climate risk management and climate investment.

* **MSMEs & Industry associations: Low carbon, water efficient technologies, climate resilient technologies and their life cycle benefits. MSMEs participants will be sensitized through 15 programmes on various technological options.**

## Activity 1.1.2: Use of Green Credit Rating Tool (DGCRIT) includes integration of Green parameters (Energy and Environment with Credit rating tool)

This activity will support the digitization of an integrated credit rating tool (to be used across national, sub-national and local levels) which includes integration of climate parameters (Energy and Environment) into credit rating tool. This tool will promote access to finance for MSMEs by integrating green criteria in the credit scoring process and promoting green technologies. In doing so, the tool will act as a risk mitigation measure for FIs – the higher the rating, the lower the risk.

The DGCRIT is an innovative tool that can be used to assess the climate initiatives within the MSME sector. Thus, such an integrated credit tool will reduce information asymmetry for FPs allowing them to better identify and support MSMEs with green initiatives.

### Implementation Arrangements

#### (a) FAA

The GCF and the Accredited Entity will enter into a single FAA (Loan and Grant), which will reflect the terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet and the NMA.

#### (b) Executing Entity

The Accredited Entity will act as an executing entity for all Activities of the Programme.

#### (c) Flow of Funds

Under the FAA, the GCF will make available to the Accredited Entity the GCF Proceeds, in the form of loan and grant, for the implementation of the Programme. The GCF Reimbursable Funds shall be provided by the GCF to the Accredited Entity pursuant to the Accredited Entity's Requests for Disbursement, subject to fulfillment of applicable conditions precedent to the Disbursements.

The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds shall be provided by the GCF to the Accredited Entity pursuant to the key terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet.

#### (d) GCF Reimbursable Funds (GCF Loan)

The Accredited Entity, acting as Executing Entity, will lend the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-financing, to the MSMEs via sub-loans. (i) loans to MSMEs and (ii) refinancing/re-hedging to FPFs for subsequent on-lending to the MSMEs.

#### Direct Loans

The Direct Loans (i.e. loans from SIDBs to MSMEs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet.

#### Indirect Loans and Sub-Loans


---
**Page 48**
---

The Indirect Loans (i.e., loans from SDBI to PFIs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet.

The Sub-Loans (i.e., loans from PFIs to MSMEs) shall be materially consistent with the key terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet.

The Accredited Entity, acting as the Executing Entity, will refinance/re-lend the GCF Reimbursable Funds, together with the Co-financing, to the PFIs. The Programme will offer refinance against eligible assets created by the empanelled PFIs and originated through the programme. This includes loans to MSMEs and other eligible borrowers as well as any other forms of lending to PFIs for creating eligible assets within stipulated time from the date of disbursement shall also be permitted on a case-by-case basis.

It is proposed to follow a three-step approach. First step is to identify and empower those eligible PFIs which have interest in climate financing and are willing to adopt new systems/processes. One of the initial conditions for empowerment would be that PFIs must have a minimum of 3 years’ experience in lending to MSMEs.

Second step is to provide sensitization and supporting handhold to these empanelled PFIs as also provide sanction of loan assistance at concessional terms along with the term sheet which will specify the terms per the FRMP programme following the guidelines issued by the GCF. The PFIs will be required to submit the relevant documents in accordance with the details outlined in the Operational Manual, Annexure 6 MSME due-diligence and Annexure 23 climate impact. The term of the indirect loan shall not exceed 10 years (maximum 75% of the term of the underlying loan). The interest rate on the Indirect Loans to PFIs under this scheme may not be issued. However, leverage of 3X will be ensured on a programme basis) of the portfolio of new MSME loans originated through the programme. PFIs would be required to accept the sanction terms and enter into loan documentation with SDBI.

The third stage would be disbursement wherein PFIs would be required to submit (or refresh) all the details, copy of the latest financial statements, copies of the latest audit reports, etc., as per the requirements of the Term Sheet. All these must be complied with. The disbursement under on-lending could be considered, upfront, based on a declaration/certification by the PFIs that they have disbursed the funds to MSMEs within 120 days (maximum upto 120 days from the date of respective disbursement) and continuing to the end use as per SDBI’s stipulations.

For avoidance of doubt, the GCF is taking the credit risk for its portion of the loan, with full recourse to SDBI. SDBI will take on the credit risk for its portion of the loan, with full recourse to each MSME under the programme.

SDBI will be managing the currency risks through appropriate hedging mechanisms and may charge risk premium to the end borrower.

The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds (GCF Grant)

The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds (GCF Grant) will be used by the Accredited Entity, acting as the Executing Entity, in order to conduct baseline/feasibility studies, capacity building and awareness, knowledge management, contract to policy development, etc., for the implementation of the Programme. The scope includes all activities related to the contract.

The GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds shall also cover the expenses of the PMU, which scope includes capacity building and monitoring of PFIs, along with the Technical Assistance activities.

There will be no on-granting of the GCF Non-Reimbursable Funds by the Accredited Entity.

### Eligibility Criteria

The eligibility criteria for all the technologies (mitigation and adaptation) financed under the programme is outlined in the Operations Manual.

#### (g) Contracting Structure

For Direct Loans, the Accredited Entity, acting in its capacity as the Executing Entity, will enter into legally binding loan agreements with the MSMEs, which will pass down the relevant GCF requirements and obligations to the MSMEs, including policy requirements set out in the A&Ms and FAA, and which shall be based on the key terms and conditions set out in the Term Sheet.

For Indirect Loans, the Accredited Entity, acting in its capacity as the Executing Entity, will enter into legally binding loan agreements with the PFIs, which will pass down the relevant GCF requirements and obligations to the PFIs, including policy

### (i) Programme Management Unit (PMU)
For smooth implementation of the programme, it is necessary to have a centralized governance and monitoring for smooth implementation of the Programme. For the project's administration and implementation, SDBI (Executing Entity) has identified a multi-vertical core team which will serve as the Programme Management Unit (PMU). The PMU, headed by the Project Director (PD), shall be responsible for the overall management of the programme including but not limited to providing guidance to the PFIs regarding assessment / compliance of climate related aspects, technical eligibility norms, environment, social due diligence and other relevant parameters of the sub loans. The PMU shall not be involved in origination, credit due diligence, credit appraisal, KYC, credit approval, loan disbursement, monitoring and reporting on the progress of the PFIs programmes and activities. The PMUs and PFIs will utilise their own staff for all these activities. For better clarification, it may be mentioned that the PMU staff shall not be involved in any of the above-mentioned activities. The PMU shall be a dedicated team which is created, funded, and managed by an expert who shall be the part of the PMU. The PMU shall be supported by an E&S specialist (EES) dedicated to the programme.

The PMU will be responsible for the execution of day-to-day activities of the programme and report to the AE about the progress of the RAMP. The primary responsibility of the PMU includes providing assistance to the Direct Credit Vertical (for direct lending) and PFIs in line with the GCF guidelines and the FAA. One of the core responsibilities of the PMU includes the identification of climate related aspects, technical eligibility norms, environment, social due diligence and other relevant aspects to be engaged by PFIs during the initial preparation phase and loan disbursement phases. The PMU shall engage in TA activities including capacity building and knowledge management etc as well as overall co-ordination with PFIs for the implementation of the programme. The PMU will also provide technical assistance to PFIs on the progress of the RAMP programmes and also on the Gencert or issues related to E&S (gender if any).

### Loan Approval Process

109. The Loan Approval Process is a comprehensive three-stage procedure designed to rigorously evaluate loan applications and ensure adherence to the stringent criteria. The first stage, Application and Initial Screening, is conducted at the branch counter. Applications are rejected if they do not meet the eligibility criteria or if the mandatory documentation such as climate-related, environmental, and social safeguards documents are incomplete.

110. Completed applications are forwarded to the Head of Branch for review. If approved by the Head of Branch, the application proceeds to the second stage, Due Diligence, while those that fail at this exclusion list are rejected. The Due Diligence stage is executed by the Credit Officer and involves verification of the borrower's identity (KYC), financial requirements, viability, credit due diligence and checking of ESIDQ checklist. Completion of the Due Diligence stage leads to the third stage, Project Evaluation and Approval. This stage includes climate-related, technical and ESS due diligence to ensure the soundness of the proposed project from a technical and economic perspective. The final step is the preparation of the Memorandum preparation phase. The Processing team comprising of Maker and Checker completes a comprehensive Credit Assessment Report (CAR) for submission to the Loan Sanction Committee for approval.

111. To maintain transparency and impartiality, no member involved in the Initial Application Screening and Due Diligence processes is permitted to be part of the Loan Sanctioning Committee.

### B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### B.5.1. Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a global initiative aimed at addressing climate change and its impacts on developing countries. This proposal outlines the justification for requesting funding from the GCF to support specific activities related to climate action.

#### B.5.2. Selection of Proposed Activities

The proposed financial instruments were selected in light of the projected activities and the overall financing package (i.e., what is needed, how much will be provided by other sources). The proposal explains why these activities are not currently being financed by public or private sectors alone. It also addresses market failure that may be addressed with GCF funding.

#### B.5.3. Financing Gap

The proposal details the financing gap and how it was determined. It explains whether the concessionality of the GCF financing complies with the terms of the GCF if applicable, providing a short market analysis on the pricing of similar financial instruments in the private sector.

#### B.5.4. Financial Structure

This section discusses the financial structure of the GCF investment and how it aligns with the overall requirements to make the investment viable. It also considers the financial structure and the proposed pricing within the context of minimum concessionality. The proposal highlights who benefits from this financing arrangement.

### References

Several reports have highlighted the gaps in climate finance as highlighted below:

- (a) India stores at a "leaking gap" of $10 billion to achieve its net zero emissions commitment by 2070.
- (b) Uneven distribution of climate finance source and need to leverage private investments. Of the $44 billion raised in climate finance, only about 35% came from public sources.
- (c) Over-reliance on domestic financing, with nearly 80% of net funds raised coming from domestic sources.

### Findings

As per the findings of RBI's survey and its notification dated July 27, 2021, and as indicated in RBI's Report on Money Supply (RMS), banks are increasingly focusing on climate-related lending. However, there is a need for training, sensitization, and capacity building of the banking sector to ensure that they can effectively integrate climate risk assessments into their frameworks for M&E, ESG, gender, etc.

### Catalysts in Financing the MSME Sector

107. Today, green lending towards MSMEs are restricted to a small portion of the larger lending portfolios of financial institutions in India—limited to 1% of the total book size.
108. Indian banks are reluctant to finance MSMEs in the climate space primarily due to their:
   - Lack of a risk-sharing mechanism to underwrite the risks associated with deployment of climate technologies in the MSME sector.

### Inability to create scale - individual MSMEs present very small/disaggregated opportunities - for effective adoption of various climate technologies (e.g. RE etc.)

### Under these circumstances, the landscape has witnessed the rise of medium-sized and smaller NBFCs (including those focused on Green NBFCS) that are dedicatedly financing MSMEs in the climate space. As can be observed from the figure below, these NBFCs have been able to scale up their lending activities by focusing on a specific set of medium-sized and smaller NBFCs.

#### a. The MSME-focused climate lending carried out by these new age BBB rated green NBFCS are primarily geared towards electric mobility but with increasing lending towards RE deployment particularly solar rooftop as well.

### However, these NBFCs, particularly Green focused NBFCs are stuck in a vicious cycle, inhibiting their ability to scale, as showcased in the figure below:

#### Low ratings
- Most Green NBFCS are new age hence BBB rated. Existing policies of banks like SBI state a minimum requirement of A.

#### Low scale
- NBFCs need to raise more capital to avail next round of equity capital to scale AJM

#### Stuck in 'Vicious Cycle'
- Limited access to capital: NBFCs need to grow to a larger ALM (1000 Cr.) to unlock SBI Funding

### Consultations with Green NBFCS have revealed the need for an ecosystem enabling environment that supports across the following areas:

a. Co-leading partnerships with medium/small NBFCs to avoid last mile reach
b. On-bonding partnerships with medium/small NBFCs against a defined use of proceeds (e.g., financing grid-connected RE)
c. Partnering with global DFIs to effect the development of a dedicated co-lending infrastructure to aid discovery and execution of co-leading deals.
d. Developing a securitization policy to play a market making role for securitization of climate assets all activities which are currently being undertaken by SBI NBFCs who now extend debt lines of capital to these medium-sized NBFCs on the back of the market created by them.

### Through this programme, SIDBI intends to scale up an ecosystem enabling environment to help scale medium/large NBFCS with dedicated climate lending towards MSMEs.

## For MSMEs, deployment of climate technologies and interventions are primarily a question of their ability to drive long-term benefits from these investments, which is in turn dependent on the availability of concessional lines of credit to finance them. The significance and necessity of these technologies, are unable to deploy primary due to lack of:

### A. Access to financing: MSMEs are typically not able to access financing for general purpose projects (MSEMs) which makes climate investments unviable – for example, consultations will find that the majority of MSEMs are not able to access finance from banks or other financial institutions because the customers are unable to purchase new EE machines and shall continue to resort to purchasing older second-hand machines which are not energy efficient but are more affordable.

### B. Inappropriate policies and business practices that circumvent the need for large CAPEX investments (as is the norm with infrastructure projects) or risk guarantees if they fail while providing more attractive norms for MSEMs, which are typically small scale and have limited access to finance.

### C. On a broader sectoral level, a clear understanding of the on-ground, real-world benefits of any climate technology especially in terms of financial benefits (including a translation of cost savings into potential revenue generation) is often lacking. This makes the investment question attractive thus activating the “Neighbourhood Effect” among the wider segments.

### 111. SIDS through this programme intends to tackle all of these challenges by creating a range of services through the concessional funding mechanism:

- Direct lending to select MSMEs to showcase creation of the real-world benefits of deploying climate technologies, which will in turn disseminate and discovering success stories across the landscape to activate the “Neighbourhood Effect”
- Leverage wider Wi-Fi network to create a much larger ecosystem of concessional lending towards MSEMs adopting energy efficient equipment and technology. This will also help in creating awareness among MSEMs as well as creating new financial products that are designed specifically to meet the needs of the MSME sector
- Enable deployment of innovative facilities such as RESCOs or co-bill financing through DISCOMs, etc. designed to create a market for energy efficient and energy MSMEs for climate technologies

## Concessionality of the funding investment

To encourage the industry and businesses to choose energy-efficient equipment, which in many cases, is more expensive than conventional equipment, concessional lending for energy efficient equipment. Although energy-efficient equipment may become economical on lifecycle cost basis, equipment buyers are most likely to decide solely based on investment cost, resulting in choosing cheaper but less efficient equipment. The proposed concessional funding mechanism will provide a lower interest loan to the buyers so that total payment amount for energy efficient equipment (equipment cost and financial cost) is comparable with conventional equipment.

It is felt that there is a need of 1% to 3% concessionally in the interest rate in order to break-even the additional cost of energy-efficient equipment. The difference between conventional and efficient technologies is not only related to environmental and social management framework. The price difference between conventional and efficient vehicles ranges from 20-50%.

Similarly, for EV there is a need of 3% to 5% concessionally in the interest rate in order to break-even the additional cost of electric vehicle. The difference between conventional and efficient technologies is not only related to environmental and social management framework. The price difference between conventional and efficient vehicles ranges from 20-50%.

In case of adoption, most Indian companies are yet to completely grasp the implications of climate change and how it will affect them. This is particularly true for the Indian MSME sector where managing the immediate challenges of

## Creating a market for long-term climate lending to MSMEs

The programme aims at designing an enabling FIs landscape to increasingly finance climate technologies and services provided by the MSMEs by creating a market for climate lending for MSMEs.

To do so, the programme shall create an enabling environment that helps FiS understand the Indian MSME sector, particularly in terms of its capacity to adopt climate technologies. This will be achieved through training, knowledge sharing and awareness building, provision of credit risk assessment tools, etc., all to be deployed to help Indian FIs understand and manage the risks associated with the Indian MSME landscape. Furthermore, detailed databases containing information on the various climate technologies that are available in India as well as their respective costs, benefits, risks and benefits of the climate technologies that they are financing.

The programme will also aim at creating a market for climate lending offered by the programme towards capitalizing small banks, NBFCs and MFIs. It is expected to create a catalytic effect and potentially leverage up-to an additional USD 240 million (to be ensured on a programme basis). Successful deployment of this large corpus towards climate technologies shall effectively contribute in creating a market for climate lending offered by the programme as well as attracting private sector financing from the wider FIs (including large banks and NBFCs) far beyond the lifecycle of the current programme.

Making climate technologies attractive for MSMEs

Furthermore, detailed databases containing information on the various climate technologies that are available in India as well as their respective costs, benefits, risks and benefits of the climate technologies that they are financing.

Successful deployment of this large corpus towards climate technologies shall effectively contribute in creating a market for climate lending offered by the programme as well as attracting private sector financing from the wider FIs (including large banks and NBFCs) far beyond the lifecycle of the current programme.

Making climate technologies attractive for MSMEs

The programme aims at designing an enabling environment that helps FiS understand the Indian MSME sector, particularly in terms of its capacity to adopt climate technologies. This will be achieved through training, knowledge sharing and awareness building, provision of credit risk assessment tools, etc., all to be deployed to help Indian FIs understand and manage the risks associated with the Indian MSME landscape. Furthermore, detailed databases containing information on the various climate technologies that are available in India as well as their respective costs, benefits, risks and benefits of the climate technologies that they are financing.

The programme will also aim at creating a market for climate lending offered by the programme towards capitalizing small banks, NBFCs and MFIs. It is expected to create a catalytic effect and potentially leverage up-to an additional USD 240 million (to be ensured on a programme basis). Successful deployment of this large corpus towards climate technologies shall effectively contribute in creating a market for climate lending offered by the programme as well as attracting private sector financing from the wider FIs (including large banks and NBFCs) far beyond the lifecycle of the current programme.

B

## Convening directly to select MSMEs to create showcases of the real-world benefits of deploying climate interventions within the MSME landscape and disseminating success stories across the landscape.

* Creating a network of MSMEs in the country that are willing to adopt climate technologies and leveraging wider Wi-Fi network to create an ecosystem of concessional lending towards MSMEs adopting climate technologies.
* Establishing a dedicated financing mechanism for climate technology adoption through DISCOMs, etc. designed to create large scale demand amongst MSMEs for climate technologies.

All of which, when deployed together creates an environment which is designed to activate the neighbourhood effect to its maximum potential and also ensures that the MSMEs are able to generate their own revenue stream (i.e., energy generation/profitability within the MSME). This ensures that going forward, beyond the life of the programme, the MSME sector remains open for the adoption of climate technologies in the long-term.

Finally, the activity implemented will be replicable at scale across the country as well as region agnostic which ensures that it is replicable, at scale within the country.


## Sustainability and Replicability of the programme

The activities suggested under the programme are designed considering their sustenance beyond programme period and is as follows:

a. The investment in around ~10000 climate loans under the programme would help in mainstreaming these amongst lenders and also ensure that the MSMEs are being used to trigger a dominant market-moving market of climate technologies especially w.r.t MSMEs. According to several studies, the following is required to scale up:

* Concessional funding which is possible through innovative financing model
* Capacity building/warning awareness generation among lending lenders
* Economic viability and affordability of a technology at large scale would lead to spillover effect. For example: large scale adoption of any technology would also ensure its market for its services

Hence, sustainability and replicability will not just be attained by provisioning loans but all the other related activities of this programme are aimed at achieving the same.

b. Also, the proposed digital integration through credit rating tool as well as use of policy instruments will further strengthen the ability of MSMEs to move towards economic and social success thus bringing such activities as part of their business priorities. This will also help the PRIs to conduct loan appraisal/financial approval of MSMEs by not just considering financial viability but also other aspects which are important for the long-term sustainability.

c. The knowledge repository developed during the project implementation will help other stakeholders including FIs and Banks, DISCOMs, etc., in understanding the climate technologies and their adoption. This will further ensure that the risk management and capacity building activities under the programme will help overcome existing “Risk Averse” nature of MSMEs wherein they tend to follow rather than leading a trend. The vendor development activity will help creating knowledge about the technology and its benefits which is essential for the successful adoption of technologies in MSMEs.

d. Transferring the lessons learned and approval of financial proposals for the proposed areas interventions will support to support green climate initiatives in the country. This will help sustainability and replicability of the programme through leveraging existing institutional arrangements and also by creating new financing instruments for MSME climate actions. SIDBI will seek additional resources from market to replenish the financing to fulfil the needs of the programme. The financial model developed as part of this programme will further be mainstreamed to enhance climate financing by banking sector for MSMEs.

e. The programme will continue functioning for climate change mitigation and adaptation for MSME sector beyond project duration to support green climate initiatives in the country. This will help sustainability and replicability of the programme through leveraging existing institutional arrangements and also by creating new financing instruments for MSME climate actions. SIDBI will seek additional resources from market to replenish the financing to fulfil the needs of the programme. The financial model developed as part of this programme will further be mainstreamed to enhance climate financing by banking sector for MSMEs.

### 3.01 The project will annually leverage USD $75 million from public and private sector investors, having a mobilization ratio of 1:6.59 towards a dedicated investment in MSMEs sector for carbon emission reduction and energy efficiency improvement. This is expected to result in an annual reduction of 243,000 tonnes CO2e, representing a reduction of 60% with allowance mitigation effects. The monitoring protocol shall describe the approach to obtain, record, communicate, and report on project results and impacts. #### Potential for Scaling Up and Replication

The programme will focus on MSME sector which is credit channel particularly in the climate space. The sub-programme typically will have low investment, high emission reduction potential. The dedicated credit and technical support approach of the programme will result in crowding-in of private funds and unlock the potential of MSMEs to scale up their adaptation and mitigation actions. The programme will also contribute towards scaling-up for adaptation and mitigation in MSMEs will accelerate industrial decarbonization trajectory of the country through (i) new technologies, (ii) energy efficiency, (iii) waste reduction, and (iv) resource recovery.

The capacity building and training of MSME / Industry Associations will generate awareness among the MSMEs on climate change adaptation and mitigation. The programme will also support the development of a knowledge hub that will disseminate best practices and lessons learned from the ecosystem of adaptation technologies which will upscale adaptation actions and build ecosystems for adoption of these technologies.

The support from the programme will unleash climate innovations, and thereby facilitate climate low carbon industrialization in MSMEs. The programme will also contribute towards scaling up of climate action in the ecosystem of adaptation technologies that will upscale adaptation actions and build ecosystems for adoption of these technologies. The programme is anchored in its comprehensive approach to integrating sustainable practices within the MSME sector through suitable policy frameworks, institutional arrangements, and enabling environment.

The programme will also contribute towards enhancing MSMEs access to sustainability practices through knowledge hubs that adopt global innovations to local needs and collaboration platforms that engage multiple stakeholders. It will promote knowledge dissemination by documenting best practices and lessons learned which

## Contribution to the Creation of an Enabling Environment

### National Climate Change Strategies and Priorities targeting MSMEs

The program is in line with the regulatory and policy frameworks governing the financial, environment, energy conservation and climate change sectors. The Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises Development Act 2006, in exclusive legislation for micro, small and medium enterprises states under its Section 9 on Measures for promoting and enhancing the competitiveness of micro, small and medium enterprises, that the government will promote the development and enhancing the competitiveness of micro, small and medium enterprises particularly through the provision of financial services. The Act also provides for the establishment of a credit rating association of micro, small and medium enterprises to provide ratings of such enterprises with a view to assessing their creditworthiness and ability to repay loans or other forms of support. Specifically, Section 10 on Credit Facility states that the policies and practices in relation to credit for the micro, small and medium enterprises shall be progressive, and such may as specified in the guidelines or instructions issued by the Bank of Jamaica.

The Government of India has been implementing overarching frameworks of National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) and National Solar Mission. The State Governments have also adopted various schemes to mitigate and adapt to climate change including Energy Efficiency, Green India, India Water Mission, and Smart Cities targeting energy efficiency and water conservation. The Pradhan Mantri Krishi Sinchai Yojana (PMKSY) scheme to achieve sustainable agriculture is proposed as a financing model for raising blended capital to finance startups for agriculture & rural infrastructure development. PMKSY is also one of the priority sectors in national financial inclusion strategy. The Government of India (RBI) which has been providing credit facilities to small and medium enterprises, including those in the agricultural sector, focuses on the Small and Medium Enterprises in its ambitious climate targets for 2030 and beyond.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 61

## D

### Adapting to Climate Change

#### India's Adaptation Efforts

India has launched various pilot projects in collaboration with bilateral/multilateral agencies, including the Green Climate Fund (GCF), to address climate change impacts and enhance resilience.

- **National Water Mission**: Focuses on water conservation, minimization of water stress, and sustainable management.
- **National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change (NMKS)**: Provides real-time data on climate impacts, enabling adaptation interventions such as early warning systems against extreme weather events. Funds the NMKS through the National Adaptation Fund (NAF).
- **National Mission on Sustainable Habitats**: Strengthens habitats' ability to withstand climate change by improving water management and implementing early warning systems.
- **National Mission on Climate Proofing of Infrastructure Products**: Enhances infrastructure resilience, focusing on buildings, roads, bridges, etc.

#### Financial Mechanisms

The project will contribute to regulatory policies demonstrating sustainable practices within India. It aims to provide data for stringent environmental standards and enhance policy insights through advanced technologies focused on energy efficiency, climate house gas reduction, and water conservation. The project aligns with the Paris Agreement and national action plans on climate change, restricting the regulation of carbon emissions and promoting clean technology adoption.

#### Conclusion

The project sets a foundational precedent for future sustainable initiatives within the regulatory landscape.

## Additional legislation and priorities that the programme supplements

### 145 The Energy Conservation (SCE) Act, 2001, accommodates production utilization of energy and its preservation for matters associated therewith or incidental thereof. It was instituted by the Parliament in the Fifty-second Year of the Republic of India and was enacted on 29th September 2001 but came into practice from 31st March, 2004.

### 146 Environment Protection Act, 1986, as amended thereon, relates to the protection and improvement of the human environment including air, water, soil, and land. It was enacted on 5th January 1986.

### The Act is an "umbrella" legislation designed to provide a framework for central government coordination of the activities under control and state authorities established under previous texts.

#### 145 In addition to the above, this programme is also aligned to the resolve of the Government of India to achieve the following:

- Reach 800GWh non-fossil energy capacity by 2030.
- So that 60% of total projected electricity comes from renewable energy by 2030.
- Reduction of total projected carbon emissions by one billion tonnes from now to 2030.
- Reduction in the per capita consumption of electricity to 450 per cent for 2025, over present levels.
- Achieving the target of net zero emissions by 2070.

#### 146 Further, in the very recent G20 Delhi declaration, the leaders unanimously accepted the recommendation of the World Bank’s Global Commission on the Economy and Climate to accelerate the transition towards low-carbon, inclusive models such as blended finance, to leverage private funds and mainstream climate funds.

# Co-benefits:

* **Job Creation / Employment opportunities through low-cost climate resilient finance assistance:** There are also significant co-benefits in terms of job creation and employment opportunities for the economy as well as generation jobs. The employment generation per million of investment in climate energy in India stands at 10,524 jobs.
* **Partnerships with technical experts / ESCOs to bring in much needed expertise through joint ventures and sell associated products:** This will also help in building a strong network of ESCOs and other stakeholders.
* **Further policy measures (a) Digital Integrated Climate Credit rating tool: (b) Assessment and recommendation for carbon credits:** These will further strengthen the climate energy sector.

## Environmental and climate benefits:

Energy efficiency in MSMEs can play a major role in reducing local air pollution, which is mainly caused by incomplete combustion of fossil fuels such as diesel, kerosene, hydrocarbons and nitrogen oxides. In doing so, it can lead to a range of associated economic, environmental and health benefits. The Project will enable expected savings more than 275 million kWh energy and ~1,458,641 million kg CO₂ emissions.

## Better productivity & Quality:

- Enhanced EE machines improves competitiveness, product quality, material efficiency, and reduces operating costs.
- Energy efficiency can improve the overall productivity of a plant by up to 30% and operational benefits derived can be up to 2.5 times the value of the energy savings (IEA, 2014b).
- Improved bottom line "profitability": Cost-effective investments in energy efficiency generate energy savings that have been estimated at $8 billion per year for India's MSMEs.
- Reduced Credit Risk: Energy efficiency can also help mitigate risks for MSMEs. While risk is inherent in all credit decisions, the risk of default on loans to MSMEs is higher than other sectors due to their exposure to credit markets, given their limited financial base. MSMEs often face more severe credit conditions than larger enterprises. Banks may not be as motivated to engage with MSMEs, as the transaction costs of smaller loans are proportionally higher.
- Women Empowerment: Various TA activities including capacity building and financial literacy of women workers/worker's rights representatives will be conducted so that they can become an independent entrepreneur. On the other hand, women entrepreneurs will be attracted for credit incentives in terms of interest Subventions.

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 64

Initiatives will help in increasing the existing women employment ratio in MSMEs. As per the GCF gender policy, the Project will implement Gender Assessment and Action Plan as per Annexure D submitted with the Full Proposal.

Country needs
India is entering a dynamic phase in energy development post-Covid19, according to the India Energy Outlook 2021 report. It highlights the unprecedented surge in appliance, air conditioning, and electric vehicle (EV) adoption. The country's power sector will need to transition from coal-based generation to its power system to the size of the European Union. Key to economic growth will be affordable energy access for all, which requires significant investments in grid infrastructure advancements, and increased clean energy investments. Plans include quadrupling renewable electricity by 2030, doubling natural gas use, enhancing energy efficiency and infrastructure, boosting EV adoption, and reducing emissions intensity by 45% by 2030 from 2005 levels under the Paris Agreement.

India's vulnerability to climate change was emphasized in the 2015 Paris Agreement. Designing a resilient energy system is critical for India as it faces significant challenges such as the Himalayan region. A World Bank study suggests that unchecked emission growth could impact the livelihoods of over 30 million people by 2050, with an additional 47 million facing overall emission increases necessitating large investments to mitigate climate change effects like altered rainfall, frequent droughts, groundwater depletion, and rising sea levels, which threaten environmental sustainability. The World Bank's Climate Change Knowledge Network (CCKN), the Environment, and Water (CEEW) show that over 80% of India’s population resides in districts with high vulnerability to climate change impacts. These areas are highly vulnerable, followed by eastern, western, northern, north-eastern, and central zones. Several states have already declared a state of emergency due to droughts. The government aims at reducing emission intensity by 45% by 2030 from 2005 levels under the Paris Agreement. This could lead to 95% emissions increase from 2014 levels by 2030, yet India's efforts are aligned with its climate goals. The government has set a target of reducing emission intensity to meet its climate targets. Meeting these targets will require an estimated $2.1 trillion in spending over the next decade. To achieve this goal, India needs to invest $57 billion annually and needed $170 billion annually. This underscores the importance of innovative financing and technologies in advancing India's climate goals.

# Needs of MSMEs

Meeting the India’s aspirations related to climate change mitigation and adaptation through easy and affordable finance to support its efforts. These efforts have been supported by various National and international agencies e.g., GEF, UNDP, The World Bank, KfW, AFD, etc., by means of financial and technical assistance. The focus has been on MSMEs only and not specific to MSMEs only. Further, many of these have focused on Climate Mitigation only and not on adaptation. The list of technologies that are considered climate resilient is on the higher side presently, making such projects unviable.

## Specific needs of MSMEs in terms of Climate Change Mitigation:

- **Reduction in Energy Consumption:** MSMEs are significant contributors to carbon emissions, particularly the energy-intensive subsectors. There is a critical need to adopt low-carbon technologies and improve energy efficiency.
- **Enhanced Energy Efficiency:** The adoption of energy-efficient technologies that are less efficient and more polluting. Upgrading these technologies is essential for reducing energy consumption and improving energy efficiency.
- **Access to Climate Finance:** MSMEs often face barriers in accessing finance for climate upgrades. There is a need for financial products tailored to the unique challenges and risks of the sector.
- **Capacity Building and Awareness:** MSMEs require training and awareness programs on how to adopt low-carbon technologies, energy-efficient practices, and other mitigation options.

## Policy Support: Supportive policies and incentives are needed to drive the adoption of climate technologies and practices in the MSME sector.

### Climate Change Adaptation Needs:

- **Risk Assessment and Management:** MSMEs need tools to support access to management and financial instruments, particularly those related to natural hazards like water scarcity, floods, and extreme weather events.
- **Infrastructure Upgrades:** Investments are necessary to make infrastructure more resilient against climate impacts, which includes upgrading facilities and adopting resilient construction practices.
- **Diversification of Water Sources:** Given the dependency on monsoon rains, there is a need for MSMEs to diversify their water sources and increase their storage capacities to buffer against drought conditions.
- **Enhanced Information Access:** MSMEs need better access to climate forecasts and related information to inform informed decisions regarding crop planting, water use, and other critical business operations.
- **Community and Ecosystem Resilience:** Programs that enhance the overall resilience of the community and ecosystem are essential for protecting local water sources and ecosystems that support agriculture and other MSME activities.

### Further MSMEs can contribute towards the manufacturing/servicing of adaptations technologies and products to help build climate resilience in vulnerable regions like the Himalayas, coastal and eastern belt in the sectors of agriculture, water resources, disaster risks, agroforestry and services. The tentative list of technologies are outlined below:

- **Weather forecasting and early warning systems:** To identify the climate-related natural hazards that may impact their operations.
- **Software application:** Consumer software applications and enterprise software applications on mobile devices concerning climate-related services.

#### Describe the following:
* Existing national climate strategy
* Existing GCF projects programme
* Alignment with existing policies such as Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), Nationally Appropriate Mitigation Actions (NAMAs), and National Adaptation Plans (NAPs)
* Capacity of Accredited Entities to Enabling Entities to deliver
* Role of the private sector
* Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, women and other vulnerable groups

### 147. The Government of India has been implementing overarching framework of National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC) for achieving climate change mitigation and adaptation goals. NAPCC is a comprehensive strategy to support the implementation of mitigation and adaptation efforts. Under its National Mission on Energy Efficiency, the Government of India is implementing various schemes to promote energy efficiency in all sectors including industry, transport, buildings, and consumers targeting only major high energy consuming industries. The Performance Limited Incentive Scheme is promoted for promoting energy efficient technologies in the industrial sector. The scheme aims at providing financial support required for farm value chain. MSME sector is also one of the priority sectors in national financial inclusion strategy. India has a large number of small and medium enterprises (SMEs) which are the backbone of the economy, especially needed by vulnerable groups such as weaker sections and low-income groups on an affordable cost by mainstream institutions. The government has launched various schemes to promote MSME sector including credit lines for micro-sized enterprises. India's climate actions, economic development and financial inclusion strategies are aligned with the proposed projects focus on MSME carbon development pathways and accelerating climate adaptation actions.

### 148. In line with ambitious climate targets for 2030 and net-zero pathways for 2070, India has also co-launched Leadership Climate Action Plan (LCAP) in 2021 to achieve the goals of LCAP. The LCAP is a multi-year plan spanning over ten years to facilitate climate finance and technology transfer for industry decarbonization and energy efficiency improvement. The LCAP aims at achieving significant reduction in emissions from the industrial sector, increasing productivity of the industries with emission reduction as co-benefits and often primarily targeting large industrial clusters. The LCAP also includes a comprehensive strategy on carbon capture utilization and storage (CCUS) and economy wide emission reduction strategy, focusing on providing low-cost finance and support for adoption of low carbon technologies.

## D

### The proposed program is a great priority of the NDA and is aligned with several priorities included in India's NDCs, including:

- a) moving forward and further propelling a healthy and sustainable way of living based on traditional values and conservation and inclusion, through including a mass movement for LIFE - "Lifestyle for Environment" as a key element of the program
- b) reducing Emissions Intensity of GDP by 45 percent below 2005 levels in 2030 (or 60 percent with additional efforts)
- c) achieving 100 GW of non-fossil fuel-based energy resources by 2030, with the help of transfer of technology and low-cost international financial assistance
- d) reducing the per capita carbon footprint to less than 2.5 tons of CO2 equivalent through additional forest and tree cover by 2030
- e) better adapt to climate change by enhancing resilience in agriculture, water resources, Himalayan region, coastal regions, health and disaster management, etc.
- f) mobile demonstration projects for the transfer of technology and capacity building in India
- g) taking action in view of the resource required and the resource gap; and vii) to build capacities, create domestic frameworks and institutions for the adoption of cutting-edge climate technology in India and for joint collaborative R&D for such future technologies.

### 103. India's NDC is ambitious, and it is a significant contribution towards achieving the goals of the Paris Agreement. The alignment with India's NDCs is a step forward towards our long-term goal of reaching net-zero by 2070. To align with India's NDCs, the proposed program will be implemented in phases over the next five years and beyond.

- A continued interest from NSPs with SIDBI has shown an increasing interest among NSPs in lending to MSMEs for adopting climate interventions.
- Continued handholding in the form of technical assistance is required to address the gap of information symmetry among MSMEs with regards to climate technologies.

## Funding climate technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as a risky business. While the MSMEs can borrow from other private PFIs, the concessionally provided under this programme will drive the adoption of climate technologies and help to reduce their cost of borrowing. The key challenge for the GCF in this regard is particularly challenging. To address this, the programme's key feature will be reaching MSMEs in rural areas through SDBI and its associated PFIs. This will also help to reach MSMEs that do not have access to private sector investment as it is targeting MSMEs that do not have access to alternative funding.

## The entire programme is structured by taking learnings from SDBI's previous programs related to Renewable Energy, Water and Sanitation, and Climate Change.

### 1. The climate finance portfolio of SDBI grew 16 times in FY 2023 which is expected to lead to energy savings of 530 GWh annually (equivalent to 480,000 tonnes CO2 eq).

### 2. In FY 2023-2024, financing for MSMEs by RECP has led to Electricity Savings of ~1.3 billion units annually with expected savings of 570 GWh in FY 2024-2025.

### 3. The list of 90+ mitigation technologies financed by SDBI through its various programs is attached as annexure. Thus, the best available technologies and practices have been considered and applied using SDBI's past successful experience.

## Funding multiplier effect and Estimated cost per ICo2e

### GCF contribution by way of loan and TA Grant aggregating to USD 215.6 million (USD 300 million as loan, and USD 94.6 million as TA Grant) will help SDBI to mobilize a total investment of USD 957.7 million in 5 years (SDBI – USD 800 million; NBFPCPFIs – USD 240 million; MSMEs – USD 377 million) for climate mitigation and adaptation measures, thereby improving the efficiency and effectiveness of each dollar invested by GCF.

## Co-financing Ratio of GCF loan to SDBI's loan is 1:4

### Estimated cost per ICo2e
USD 120 million (GCF contribution)/.35.34 million ICo2 eq = $3.39 USD / ICo2 eq.

## Water Savings
### Through these programmes, there will be substantial water savings through Adaptation measures by MSMEs, amounting to ~1,458,641 million litres of water (lifetime).

## GCF mobilization mechanism

### Mobilization Ratio of 7:16.59

### Based on current estimations, the GCF fund of USD 215.6 million will help SDBI to channelize a total investment of USD 957.7 million in 5 years (SDBI – USD 800 million; NBFPCPFIs – USD 240 million and another USD 377 million will be mobilized by MSMEs). The GCF fund will be used to finance the following:

- SDBI will lend around 10% of the loans directly and another 90% of the loan will be financed indirectly through PFIs from the USD 1000 million PAMF facility. SDBI will provide technical handholding support to the selected PFIs for mobilizing funds from the PAMF facility.
- The GCF fund will also be used to finance a portion of the climate finance portfolio, in line with the climate financial instruments of SDBI. SDBI will select the PFIs based on their asset portfolio and the type of financing they are able to provide.

### Further list of PFIs, SBFs and NBFCs with whom SDBI is working is attached as Additional supporting documents 2.

#### E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Monitoring and verification (MV) for verification of project indicators will be undertaken in one or two years starting from the first year onwards on a sample basis including both direct loans and PPFs to include demonstrations of sub-products like energy savings, environmental benefits e.g., CO2 emission reductions, and other co-benefits at programme level.

As an Accredited Entity, SIDBI will be responsible for developing, implementing, monitoring, and reporting the overall implementation of the programme and periodically submitting progress reports to the Funded Authorities (FA) and the Accredited Entities Agreement. The Management Information System (MIS) developed by the Project Director consists of experts shall be responsible for the execution of day-to-day activities of the programme for direct loans as well as for the loan sanctioned directly through PPFs. SIDBI will be responsible for MVAV for direct loans and for MVAV for sub-products sanctioned directly and through PPFs. PEI will ensure in-house MAV as well as MVAV through independent agencies.

Additionally, a Management Information System (MIS) will be developed to assist in project tracking, including dashboards for loan cases and online regular monitoring of the programme.

Moreover, an Impact Evaluation at the end of the project will be undertaken. Lastly, as part of the project monitoring process internal financial audit will be conducted annually. Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of Climate impacts: Mitigation and Adaptation will be achieved through the use of energy efficiency savings through MRV tools.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1

#### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Medium
- **Impact:** Medium

**Description**

The technical risk may be owing to lack of understanding by the end beneficiaries regarding a particular Resource Efficient and Cleaner Production (RECP) technology which may hinder its adoption and may affect market demand. The RECP technologies are capital intensive, and the cost of these technologies is high. The purchasing of these equipment/technologies may be hindered by lack of service providers in the market. In addition, MSMEs may be unable to invest 10%, resulting in sunk costs and reduced competitiveness in the FAMAP industry.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures have the probability of not occurring? If so, what does it?

- **Technical Assistance component includes various activities such as awareness creation, capacity building of various stakeholders and provision of technical assistance to beneficiaries.**
- **Further, the programme envisages providing end-to-end handholding support to participating units. The approach will be to provide a package of services to the beneficiary unit.**
- **The proposed activities will also support 'development' of mentors (Cluster champions or stalwarts) and will also provide peer learning opportunities for MSMEs in the same cluster.**

### Selected Risk Factor 2

#### Category: Credit
- **Probability:** High
- **Impact:** Medium

**Description**

Credit risk is the possibility of a loss happening due to a borrower's failure to repay a loan or to satisfy contractual obligations under a credit agreement. The risk may be further compounded by various factors including market crunch, loss of revenue, or any other situation beyond their control.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures have the probability of not occurring? If so, what does it?

- **The operational risks related to end-of-sale services will be mitigated by the market itself. It is understood that end-of-sale services are provided by the service providers who are also the suppliers of RECP technologies. The service providers funded under this programme, there is bound to be spillover effect in the market which would incentivise service providers to enter into their operations in the market.**


---
**Page 92**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 12.0 | PAGE 86

In line with SDBI guidelines, SDBI has developed a detailed assessment process for risk measurement following which investment decisions are taken. Some of the Risk Mitigation Measures are—

- First and foremost, the Fund is structured under the programme.
- Personal guarantee of promoter(s).
- Corporate Guarantee of group companies, if available and feasible.
- Insurance cover for the pledged assets of the Borrower, to meet asset coverage norms.
- PSC/PHASE / NCGTC cover may be obtained, wherever felt necessary.
- Further, as per SDBI’s annual report for FY22-23, the Gross NPAs and Net NPA of the Bank stood at normal levels. The high level of NPA is owing to various risk management policies of the organization, rating tools, usage of block chain technology, etc., which are in line with the industry norms.
- SDBI commits USD 800 million, through co-financing commitment letter and documented in the term sheet.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Forex | Medium | Medium |

Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time. The probability may change with respect to time. For example, there is the possibility for an entity to be affected by a variation in the exchange rate due to its local currency and the currency used in a transaction with a foreign country.

Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurrence?

The funds requested from GCF will be in USD while SDBI will lend in INR. SDBI will be managing the currency risks through appropriate hedging mechanisms and may change risk premium to the end borrowers. The indicative annual hedge cost is taken as 2.05 percent per annum and is indicated in assumptions given in Annexure J-3: Financial model.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| ML/FT | Medium | Medium |

Description
Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time. Banks and other financial institutions may be used as a channel for Money Laundering (M) / Terrorist Financing (TF); hence there is an associated risk related to ML/FT.

Mitigation Measure(s)
Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurrence?

SDBI has a well-formulated policy to prevent ML/FT with well laid out procedure to conduct risk assessment which is attached as Annexure: Yes, the mitigation measure can well reduce the possibility of a risk originating from ML/FT; (a) SDBI will ensure that all its employees are aware and trained on the risks associated with ML/FT. (b) SDBI will identify, assess and take effective measures to mitigate its money laundering and terrorist financing risk for clients, counterparties and transactions. ## Identification of environmental & social risks: Projects are assessed for their potential environmental and social impacts. This includes both negative impacts that need to be mitigated and potential benefits that can be achieved.

These plans must comply with national regulations and international standards, and reporting on environmental and social performance.

#### Grievance Redressal: This also includes a Grievance Redressal Mechanism to ensure all grievances are addressed in accordance with the ESMS.

##### Public Disclosure & Consultation: SIDS will publish public disclosure relevant reports in compliance with its Information Disclosure policy. The results of the preliminary environmental and social screening, as well as the final assessment report, must be made available for public consultation.

##### Zero Tolerance of SEAM: SIDS has ZERO TOLERANCE towards any sexual harassment in any form. SIDS has adopted the Sexual Harassment at Work (Prevention & Prohibition & Redressal) at Workplace as per the Sexual Harassment of Women (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act 2013.

##### Indigenous People: The COP decision adopting the Paris Agreement recognized the need to strengthen practices and efforts of local communities and Indigenous Peoples related to addressing and responding to climate change. SIDS will ensure that indigenous people are involved in all aspects of project development. However, given that MSMEs are restricted to areas of industrial clusters/technological zones, SIDS's project design must be inclusive of the needs of indigenous peoples.

##### Details on each of the above elements of the ESMS framework are shared below:

### Project Screening

SIDS will, or the PFAs that are engaged, will screen the projects to ensure that the proposed projects are not in the Negative Impact List prepared by SIDS and put forth per their ESMP (Annexure I of Annexure 1 of the ESMS (Annexe 4)).

The negative list includes project programmes involving harmful or exploitative form of child labour, production or use of hazardous substances, including tobacco, alcohol, drugs, etc. The inclusion of a Project on the Negative Impact List will ensure that Category A/I projects are screened out and will not be financed under this programme.

### Identification & assessment of E&S risks

SIDS will, or the PFAs that are engaged, will screen the projects to ensure that the proposed projects are not in the Negative Impact List prepared by SIDS and put forth per their ESMP (Annexure I of Annexure 1 of the ESMS (Annexe 4)).

The negative list includes project programmes involving harmful or exploitative form of child labour, production or use of hazardous substances, including tobacco, alcohol, drugs, etc. The inclusion of a Project on the Negative Impact List will ensure that Category A/I projects are screened out and will not be financed under this programme.

### Manage & Monitor Risk, Reporting & Compliance

SIDS (or PFA) is responsible for identifying environmental and social risks identified during the screening of environmental and social risks. The implementing entities' programme reports shall include a section on status of mitigation of any environmental or social risks that were identified in the project proposal and/or any measures to mitigate or mitigate these risks.

The reports shall also indicate, if necessary, a description of any corrective actions that are deemed necessary. Accountability for the implementation of such corrective action report shall also include an evaluation of Programme performance with respect to environmental and social risks.

The frequency and periodicity of submitting such reports for effective monitoring is decided during the commencement of the Programme.

### Grievance Redressal Mechanism

SIDS has a comprehensive 3-year Grievance Redressal Mechanism (GRM) (Refer Attachment 3 of ESMS Annexure 4). If it is found that the project does not meet the registered registration form for complaints, grievances, whereby the project affected stakeholders can log their grievances.

## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Provide a summary of the gender assessment and projecting/strategic-level gender action plan that is aligned with the overall project objective and strategy. Describe how the gender analysis was conducted and the process used to develop both documents. Include any relevant data, statistics, or research that has informed this assessment.

## Approach

In preparing the Gender Assessment & Action Plan for the Programme, following approaches have been leveraged:

1. Baseline assessment of participation of women in the MSE sector in India as entrepreneurs
   - a. Literature reviewed from secondary sources to understand the baseline situation and the good/bad scope for gender equality in MSEs.
   - b. Secondary data on women's entrepreneurship (e.g., number of women-owned MSEs, employment rates, financial inclusion) was collected from various reports, about national policies and guidelines (environmental, social, gender equality), historical studies and research on Entrepreneurship Development (ED), international best practices, potential ED interventions etc considering gender equality were analysed.


---
**Page 98**
---

b. This assessment has been done based on SIDBI’s existing initiatives to promote women entrepreneurship and based on research studies supported by SIDBI.

# Assessment of challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in the MSEMS sector

## a. Stakeholder consultation (e.g., Strengthening Green Financing and Manufacturing Gender) organized under the umbrella of GIFT, Women Entrepreneurs’ Empowerment through Microfinance for Women (Grow) Initiatives, founded by SIDBI; Shakti Sustainable Energy Foundation (SSEF) and French Development Agency (AFD) in partnership with SIDBI.

## b. Review of SIDBI’s existing capacities to promote women entrepreneurship/gender equality

### Key Findings & Assessment

- **Gender Parity in Participation:** Only ~24% of the workforce in MSEMS is women, which is lower than the national labor force participation rates for females (~38.7% in 2019). This suggests that MSEMS have a scope to improve their female representation.
  
- **Gender Parity in Ownership:** As per the statistical data provided on the Udyam Registration Portal (URP) under the Ministry of MSMEs, women owned MSEMS constitute 23.7 percent of the total number of registered MSEMS.

Following are some of the key challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in India:

- **Not Sustainable:** ~73% women-led enterprises have faced a reduction in revenues post COVID-19. Most female entrepreneurs are concentrated in sectors and industries highly affected by the pandemic and agricultural activities, which are seasonal in nature.
  
- **Not Autonomous:** In ~20% cases, the business-related decisions are taken by the respective spouses and not by women themselves. This is a major challenge faced by women-led enterprises as they depend considerably on men for key business decisions, procurement of raw materials, marketing, etc.

- **Not Formalized:** ~56% of the women in enterprises are informal and not registered with relevant institutional instruments. A high level of informality harms the growth and resilience of their enterprises and their entrepreneurial rights. Informal workers have no access to social security benefits such as old age pension and expose women to occupational health hazards.
  
- **Not sufficient employment generating:** ~95% of women proprietor-run units with less than six workers, most being informal, are engaged in the production of goods and services for household use.

This points to the fact that women entrepreneurs often lack skills in scaling businesses i.e. in areas such as business management, market access, access to finance, etc.
  
- **Lack of digital literacy:** ~50% of women in MSEMS have low levels of digital literacy. Women in MSMEs have been confined to low-paying, low-skilled jobs. The ongoing transitions present them with an opportunity to realign their skills and move up the value chain. Digital literacy is one of the most important skills that women need to acquire to keep pace with the ongoing transitions. This will enable them to enter traditionally male-dominated sectors like those heavily reliant on digital technologies, such as healthcare services, automobiles, and more advanced roles within the textiles sector with green technologies.

## Gender Action Plan

Based on the programme design, the broad aim is to ensure adequate representation for women as stakeholders and beneficiaries under the project. This will be achieved through a combination of financial support (under component 2), for capacity development, awareness generation, etc., as well as in MAE frameworks; and leveraging existing initiatives such as the Women’s Empowerment Project (WEP) and the Microfinance Institutions Network (MIN).

A stated endeavor of the programme is to secure 80-20 coverage (i.e. 25% financing towards women), broadly in line with the selected baselines for women's ownership and labour force participation. In the longer term, the aim is to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) by increasing women’s economic empowerment, gender roles/workforce participation in agriculture, as well as other benefits from climate mitigation and adaptation activities taken up in the MIN. The project will also ensure that the needs of women entrepreneurs are addressed and thus the landscape of women-led MSMEs in India shall be significantly transformed.

### Follow-up Actions

- **Number of women led MSMEs:** Increase the number of women-owned MSMEs through necessary interventions and ensure enhanced participation of women workers in MSMEs.
- **Business development services (BDS):** Provide BDS to women-led enterprises/MSMEs. Make women-led enterprises/MSMEs more sustainable & resilient to external shocks, especially climate shocks by providing access to affordable finance for mitigation/adaptation activities. The technologies provided by the project program shall be available and accessible to both women and men.
