# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP241

## Proposal name: Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in Indian MSMEs

### Accredited entity: Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI)

### Country/ies: India

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

The Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Project (FMAP) aims to accelerate the adoption of low-emission and climate-resilient practices among micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs). The project is implemented in India by SIDBI as the accredited entity (AE) and executed jointly with partners. The core objective of FMAP is on financing adaptation projects, which have so far seen limited uptake by banks and financial institutions.

The project will focus on three main components:

1. **First component:** A USD 2 billion lending facility managed by SIDBI, leveraging the GCF loan of USD 700 million from the GCF. These funds will support direct lending (20 per cent) to MSMEs for climate-aligned initiatives and indirect financing (80 per cent) through PFIs as co-financing partners.

2. **Second component:** A USD 16 million grant facility managed by SIDBI, leveraging a USD 500 million loan from the GCF. These grants will support MSMEs in generating their operations by identifying the technical needs and capacity gaps of their enterprises and contributing to policy initiatives. SIDBI will prioritize conducting technology need assessments to identify the most pressing challenges faced by MSMEs and provide targeted technical assistance on loans through a dedicated project management unit.

3. **Third component:** A USD 2 billion lending facility managed by PFIs, leveraging a USD 500 million loan from the GCF. These funds will support MSMEs in generating their operations by identifying the technical needs and capacity gaps of their enterprises and contributing to policy initiatives. SIDBI will prioritize conducting technology need assessments to identify the most pressing challenges faced by MSMEs and provide targeted technical assistance on loans through a dedicated project management unit.

The programme seeks to support constrained sectors, particularly in adaptation, by providing concessional lending and technical assistance.


---
**Page 108**
---

1.2 Impact potential

Scale: Medium to high

India is a major emitter of greenhouse gases (GHGs). Given its rapid economic development and substantial population growth, it is imperative for India to pursue a development path that leads to reduced emissions intensity. The Paris Agreement requires developed countries to provide funding and initial adaptation communication submitted by India under the Convention in December 2015, laid out a roadmap for achieving this goal. India’s total GHG emissions are 3.4 gigatonnes equivalent (Gt CO₂e), reflecting a 4.56 per cent increase from the 2015 level and a 115 per cent increase since 1990. CO₂ remains the main GHG, accounting for 79 per cent of total emissions in India, followed by methane (CH₄) at 13 per cent and nitrous oxide (N₂O) at 8 per cent.

The funding proposal findings explain that India’s energy-intensive MSME subsectors are substantial contributors of GHG emissions. However, MSMEs face several barriers to becoming more climate-aligned, including their use of conventional processes and technologies coupled with limited access to finance and technology. These barriers are exacerbated by the increasing demand for waste generation and emissions. There are over 63 million MSMEs in India, employing about 110 million people. The MSME sector is a major contributor to GHG emissions, accounting for approximately 25 per cent of total energy used by the industrial sector in India, representing an estimated 470 million tonnes of CO₂e annually. By transitioning from fossil fuels and water, in a cost-effective manner, MAP aims to support this transition by improving access to finance and technology.

Considering the above, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) takes a positive view of FMAP’s main goal of sensitizing MSMEs towards adopting green and cleaner processes and technologies. The TAP acknowledges that FMAP is in line with India’s NDC, which aligns with the programme's aim to encourage banks and financial institutions to develop dedicated green financing products for MSMEs. This will help MSMEs in India to better adapt and developing the necessary green technologies. Transitioning MSMEs in India to lower carbon, more resilient pathways could be a crucial contributor to helping India meet its climate goals.

FMAP will provide loans to MSMEs targeting the renewable energy, energy efficiency and low-carbon technology sectors. In addition, it will support adaptation by helping MSMEs in the water-intensive and agri-food sectors to adopt new technologies and practices, thereby increasing resilience and adaptive capacity against climate change. Adaptation investment needs are estimated at $38 billion (Rs 2 trillion) for the next five years across the eastern belt of India. According to the funding proposal, FMAP is expected to cost $43.6 million ($0.5 billion). The TAP notes that this is a large and long-term (13 years) challenge due to uncertainties in the composition of MSMEs’ emissions and their future energy demand. Given these projections in the context of FMAP; however, the TAP acknowledges that the targeted loan areas (e.g., energy, water, agriculture) are consistent with India’s climate change adaptation science mechanism means that there is the potential for significant impact. Importantly, the sheer scale of India and the role of MSMEs in the economy support an argument that the overall impact will be substantial.

Moreover, FMAP is expected to mobilize USD 3.5 billion from public and private sector sources, which represents a remarkable emissions reduction of approximately 100 million tonnes of CO₂e annually by 2030. This would represent a significant reduction in MSMEs’ emissions and contribute towards India’s NDC. Of this, the funding proposal estimates that 40 per cent of the funding will address adaptation actions, and the remaining 60

The proposal also effectively argues that a key barrier to climate action is the lack of access to affordable finance. IFAP will provide concessional loans for MSMEs to develop green projects, including renewable energy solutions. By enhancing the capacity of MSMEs and financial institutions for green financing

## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: Medium to high

The FMAP funding proposal estimates that the program will directly reduce GHG emissions by 18 million tonnes through approximately 10,000 green loans to MSMEs over its 13-year life. The proposed loan adoption rate of 25%, which is expected to help MSMEs save over 100,000 GWh of energy and 1,241 million cubic meters of water compared with business-as-usual (BAU) levels.

To support the program's climate change mitigation goals, the proposal projects ancillary environmental benefits such as cleaner air and reduced water pollution. The FMAP funding proposal estimates that MSMEs can significantly reduce local air pollutants, particularly outdoor concentrations of particulate matter (PM10).

The funding proposal credibly demonstrates FMAP’s potential to contribute to India's sustainable development beyond mitigation and adaptation actions.

### Economic co-benefits


---
**Page 111**
---

The provided climate financing is expected to stimulate economic activity and create over 100,000 jobs. Low investment costs for implementing green technologies are anticipated to directly enhance the profitability of MSMEs through increased productivity and growth.

The programme will also target innovative entrepreneurs to drive MSME growth, positively impacting India's gross domestic product, given the sector’s economic significance.

Cost-effective investments in energy efficiency will generate energy savings that directly improve the profitability of MSMEs and provide net positive earnings in the long run. Reduced costs can be reinvested into new productive activities such as expanding or pursuing new product lines or services; and

Social and general benefits:

The adoption of clean technologies and best practices will support increased access to affordable clean energy and water-efficient technologies, improving the health and well-being of communities.

The programme will empower women and ensure their adequate representation as stakeholders and beneficiaries. The funding proposal specifies that 20 per cent of the total amount will be allocated for this purpose.

Overall, the ITAP rates the sustainable development potential of FMAP as medium-high.


### Needs of the recipient: Scale: Medium to high

India is experiencing rapid economic growth and entering a new phase of energy development, which will shape its emissions profile for many years to come. This profound impact affects both the supply and demand sides of energy. Economic growth is driving increased demand for electricity in India, with projections indicating that the country’s demand could double over the next two decades; to meet electricity demand, India must expand its power system on a scale comparable to the entire European Union.

It is essential that this economic growth is fueled by clean, reliable energy. To achieve this, climate-aligned technologies need to be adopted across the economy, particularly by MSMEs. The Indian government has set ambitious targets for reducing emissions intensity (by 45 per cent below 2005 levels) and absolute emissions (by 35 per cent below 2005 levels) by 2030, especially in energy-intensive subsectors. Achieving a low-carbon future for India will necessitate the adoption of climate-aligned technologies across all sectors, particularly among MSMEs. However, MSMEs often face barriers in accessing finance for green upgrades. There is a pressing need for financial products tailored to the unique challenges and risks of the MSME sector.

Under the Paris Agreement, India has committed to reducing emissions intensity by 45 per cent below 2005 levels by 2030 and absolute emissions by 35 per cent below 2005 levels. The country’s total investment gap is estimated at USD 2.5 trillion, highlighting the significant investment gap with current green finance flows well below the USD 170 billion needed annually. This underscores the critical importance of innoventive financing solutions for MSMEs.

India is also highly vulnerable to climate change, as emphasized in its nationally determined contributions (NDCs). The country faces a range of challenges, including frequent and severe coastal events and fragile Himalayan regions. India ranks seventh on the Climate Risk Index and thirty-first on the INSMAP Risk Index, both of which measure climate change vulnerability. The funding proposal will support MSMEs in adapting to these risks by providing insurance against extreme weather events such as floods, droughts and cyclones, especially MSMEs dependent on agriculture located in vulnerable regions. This sector faces a number of barriers, such as limited


---
**Page 112**
---

access to technology, limited capacity to adopt new technologies and lack of affordable financing options.

29. In India, MSMEs need tools and support to assess, manage and forecast climate risks, particularly those related to natural hazards (e.g., water scarcity, floods and extreme weather events). This includes the ability to adapt to climate change impacts, including upgrading facilities and adopting resilient construction practices.

30. The FMAP is aligned with India’s NDCs and is consistent with national adaptation plans and the national climate strategy. The focus on sectors such as renewable energy, water resources management, agriculture, and forestry reflects areas of greatest vulnerability identified in India's NDC. The ITAP considers the proposed programme's emphasis on MSME mitigation and adaptation interventions by providing long-term concessional lending.

31. In addition to technical assistance provided to MSMEs to increase awareness of available climate finance opportunities, FMAP will also deliver climate impact projects that are unlikely to be implemented without FMAP.

32. The ITAP rates the needs of the recipient as medium-high.


### 1.6 Country ownership

Scale: Medium to high

32. The FMAP aligns with the implementation of India's NDC and is consistent with national adaptation plans and the national climate strategy. The focus on sectors such as renewable energy, water resources management, agriculture, and forestry reflects areas of greatest vulnerability identified in India’s NDC. The ITAP considers the proposed programme's emphasis on MSME mitigation and adaptation interventions by providing long-term concessional lending.

33. India has issued a non-objection letter for FMAP. In addition, the AE (NDB) is an arm of the Government of India and works closely with the Ministry of Finance, NABARD, and local financial institutions. These factors collectively indicate strong government engagement and national ownership.


### 1.7 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium to high

34. The ITAP finds it commendable that SIDBI will contribute a substantial portion (80 per cent) of the finance in the lending facility from its own balance sheet, demonstrating confidence in the programme’s potential for success.

35. This is also reflected in the fact that SIDBI's financial commitment is limited to the loan facility and does not extend to the grant component. The ITAP notes that this approach may be a barrier (at least initially) to MSMEs (personnel) to some capacity-building efforts, the lack of any SIDBI budget at stake in the grant component increases the risk of non-delivery of the intended impact.

36. The ITAP also notes positively that FMAP builds on SIDBI's past experiences with direct lending to MSMEs and collaboration with PFIs to promote MSME’s adoption of green technologies. This is a positive step, as it demonstrates that SIDBI has already provided significant technical assistance to bridge the information gap. We observe however that SIDBI has incorporated several innovative elements in its approach, including the requirement for PFIs to co-lend alongside FMAP.

37. The ITAP also appreciates that the FMAP development process included stakeholder consultations with both potential borrowers and the banking community in India.


---
**Page 113**

### The GCF’s focus on transitioning MSMEs to a low-carbon and more resilient pathway as an effective strategy, given the overall size of the Indian economy, the central role of MSMEs and the rapid changes occurring in India.

The proposal's aim is to deliver high-value mitigation and adaptation co-benefits through the GCF. The ability of private climate loans to MSMEs is viewed by ITAP as positive, and greening the existing financial systems of local banks and microfinance institutions (MFIs) is seen as a key priority for scaling up green finance in remote/rural areas of India, which can be particularly challenging.

To address this, the proposal aims to build capacity within the GCF Secretariat and with local financial institutions with a wider presence. This inclusive structure is crucial for developing the capacity and sustainability of climate finance in India.


### Capacity building for local financial institutions is seen as critical for delivering the expected climate impact. This will require the AIU to maintain strict screening criteria for its direct loans and effective oversight of its PBI investments.

The proposal also aims to ensure that the funding mechanisms they meet a minimal standard of acceptability but could be improved. In particular, standards are only as effective as their implementation. In this case, SIDBI serves as an example of how standards can be implemented effectively. The proposal suggests that other institutions, such as the AE acting as the GCF's fiduciary eyes and ears.

The PBI encourages the Secretariat to consider whether the current funding mechanisms for MSMEs are used effectively, minimizing the risk of merely supporting PBI balance sheets or financing interventions that do not align with the GCF’s goals.


### The USD 200 million senior loan provided by GCF will be blended with SIDBI's USD 800 million and further leveraged by 16 times through participating local financial institutions, supplementing the USD 3.5 billion in total funding that is expected to be mobilized.

Specifically, the funding proposal states that GCF funding will help SIDBI channel a total investment of USD 2.7 billion into MSMEs over five years, with an average annual growth rate in a mobilization rate of 1:20.71. This represents significant co-finance by multiple partners, including public and private sector entities, which is critical for the success of the GCF.

By requiring timely inputs from all relevant parties, FMAP has the potential to scale up green financing for MSMEs, delivering climate impact projects. In addition, partnering with local financial institutions will help ensure that the funding mechanisms are well understood and aligned with deployment.


### The green technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as risky. While MSMEs can borrow from other private financial institutions, the concessionality provided under this programme will drive the adoption of green technologies in MSMEs.

The GCF funds will enable FMAP to provide technical assistance and capacity-building for both MSMEs and participating financial institutions. The grant programme will also include a technology assessment for MSMEs. This component can increase awareness of climate technologies and dispel outdated perceptions about the costs and effectiveness of many clean technologies, such as solar power.

The involvement of local MFIs and SBFs is crucial in increasing their ability to extend climate finance.

Overall, the ITAP rates the efficiency and effectiveness of this programme as **medium-high**.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1. The ITAP considers that the FMAP programme aligns closely with the GCF private sector strategy and overall mission of supporting developing countries to pursue growth pathways that are aligned with a low-carbon and resilient future for the planet.
2. If executed well, FMAP will facilitate access to capital for vulnerable MSMEs and create innovative financial products and services in areas such as technology and processes. Importantly, FMAP will also aim to green the existing financial system by building capacity and establishing new partnerships with financial institutions that are best placed to serve the most vulnerable economic segments. In doing so, FMAP should mobilize large-scale funds from these sources to support the de-risking of private sector investments and supporting the greening of the financial system.
3. The ITAP considers that the combination of technical and technical assistance provided through FMAP can help to reduce the transaction costs associated with climate-related projects – associated with investments in climate change mitigation and adaptation. SIDBI has long been a market leader in providing finance for MSMEs, including those not significantly serviced by traditional lenders. The ITAP notes that SIDBI is well-positioned to be catalytic across MSME segments for both mitigation and adaptation activities.
4. Several aspects of the focus on financial inclusion are also relevant from an economic perspective, as financial inclusion can help to reduce poverty and improve access to finance. This is particularly important in India where SIDBI has a long history of providing financial support through its various schemes. The programme should be designed with a view towards ensuring that all MSMEs have access to the necessary information and resources to ensure proper buy-in from all key actors.
5. At the heart of FMAP is the recognition that addressing the climate finance challenge in larger and middle-income countries is best achieved by catalyzing private sector investment through innovative financial products and services. Local finance can operate more efficiently due to a better understanding of local implementation realities, including the specific needs of MSMEs.
6. However, the ITAP has noted some areas of concern, primarily related to the operationalization of FMAP over time. The FMAP concept is strong, but it relies heavily on SIDBI’s capacity in underwriting and managing climate-related projects. This could be a risk for large-scale project mitigation and adaptation impacts. Areas of FMAP that can be strengthened are noted through this assessment. FMAP should also consider the need to provide technical assistance to engineers to adapt climate-resilient technologies and solutions. In such circumstances, there could be a temporary increase in transaction costs. It is therefore important to consider additional safeguards in the operational plan to mitigate this risk and uphold the climate imperative of FMAP.
7. Summarizing the foregoing considerations, the ITAP recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP241)

## Proposal name: Financing Mitigation and Adaptation Projects (FMAP) in Indian MSMEs

### Accredited entity: Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI)
### Country/ies: India
### Project/Programme size: Large

AE duly notes the comments of ITAP and appreciates the efforts of the team in reviewing the Funding Proposal document in detail. The comprehensive feedback would help the AE in further strengthening the proposal and programme. It may be noted by the ITAP that the Funding Proposal has been prepared with due consideration to all the concerns/feedback/recommendations given by the ITAP members, which has already been factored in the FP document. AE is committed to providing further feedback and would like to give below clause wise explanation on the comments by ITAP.

## Impact potential

### ITAP’s feedback (Point 8): FMAP will provide loans to MSMEs targeting the renewable energy, energy efficiency and low-emission transport sectors. In addition, it will support adaptation measures in the context of climate change, including the adoption of resilient climate solutions in their businesses, thereby increasing resilience and adaptive capacity against climate change impacts. The targeted areas are those most vulnerable to these impacts such as coastal areas, the Himalayas and the eastern belt of India. According to the funding proposal, this will benefit 10 million people directly and another 50 million indirectly.

The ITAP notes that estimating GHG reductions over such a large programme and long tenor (13 years) is difficult. The overall impact on emissions from the FMAP portfolio is therefore difficult to put very much stock on specific projections in the context of FMAP; however, the ITAP acknowledges that the targeted loan areas (e.g., energy efficiency, low-emission transport sectors) are those most vulnerable to climate change impacts and there is the potential for significant impact. Importantly, the sheer scale of India and the role of MSMEs in India strongly support an argument that the overall impact will be positive from a climate standpoint.

### SIDBI Response:

While estimating the GHG emissions, AE has considered the following:
(i) AE has used the approved methodologies (CDM and SEC) for calculation of energy savings and GHG emission reductions of different sub-sectors.
(ii) All the projects are in line with the government’s policy on energy efficiency in MSME since 2005-06. The ITAP list comprises of IPP1 (clean, green, energy efficient) projects which have been selected after due diligence by AE and included expert agencies, Bureau of Energy Efficiency etc., AE has used the data from the projects assisted in the past, by AE for the baseline calculations.


---
**Page 116**
---

However, considering the various limitations as well as vast heterogeneity of the MSME sector, it agrees with the observations of the ITAP that although realistic estimations of GHG emissions is difficult for such a long term but the programme has a huge potential for significant impact on the most vulnerable segment i.e. MSMEs.

### ITAP feedback (Point 123)
FMAP intends not only to increase the availability of finance for climate-related loans to MSME but also use concessionality to accelerate the transition to climate-friendly technologies. The AI has made a credible case for the levels of concessionality that can be provided by GCF and the ITAP agrees with this. More importantly, to encourage MSMEs to adopt technology changes that enhance their resilience to climate impacts, the level of concessionality will be determined on a case-by-case basis. The level of concessionality will be determined over time. The ITAP recommends that SBHD regularly evaluates the impact of the programme and makes adjustments in the level of concessionality which could generate windfall gains for some PFIs while crowding out other players.

### SBHD Response
The concerns regarding determination of levels of concessionality and passing of the same over a period is duly noted and the explanation is as follows:
(a) The concessionality provided by GCF on blended basis shall be passed on to the borrower through the interest rate. The interest rate for each tranche of drawdown will be equal to the concessionality worked out on a blended basis towards each tranche of drawdown from GCF. This means that the interest rate would be determined on a blended basis to both end borrowers.
(b) All duly notes the suggestion of ITAP regarding the need to evaluate and adjust concessionality in case of over-subscription. In this regard, all sub-loans that are sold loans will undergo credit rating and the effective interest rate for the end borrower would be the interest rate determined by the credit rating agency. The credit rating parameters include various factors on obligor and facility, as well as sectoral outlook.
(c) The programme has also inbuilt suitable checks and balances which includes on sample monitoring of MSMEs to ensure that the concessionality is passed on and passing on the concessionality to the ultimate borrowers etc., to avoid over-subscription or PFIs getting windfall profits. It is expected that over time, there will be competition among three PFIs but with around 25 to 25 percent it is expected that over the period gradually there will be increased competition among these PFIs for climate financing. This would obviate the risk of windfall profits in the PFIs and shall ensure further benefits to the ultimate borrowers i.e. MSMEs.

### ITAP feedback (Point 13)
In addition, the ITAP has some concerns regarding the risk that a substantial portion of FMF funding could end up refinancing existing PFI portfolios instead of providing new financing for additional projects in the MSME sector. There is very little climate-directed utility using GCF concessionality to refinance existing portfolios. The ITAP recommends that SBHD should consider carefully whether it may wish to fund some portfolio refinance in the early stages of the programme as a way to attract PFIs and encourage them to participate. The ITAP does not necessarily accept this argument but strongly recommends that the Secretariat and SBHD develop safeguards to ensure that the bulk of concessional funding is truly for additional projects. The ITAP


---
**Page 117**
---

encourages SIDBI to develop a plan for FMAP that minimizes portfolio refinancing over time on a trajectory to zero over the programme’s tenure.

**SIDBI Response**: AE wish to state that ITAP concerns are addressed in the revised Funding Proposal and its Terms of Reference. Further, AE would like to supplement with the following w.r.t refinancing of existing portfolios:

(A) The Programme will not cover any sub-loans prior to the date of effectiveness of FMAP.

(B) The Programme will offer onlending/ refinance support against eligible assets created by the empanelled PFIs and originated no longer than six months prior to the date of effectiveness of FMAP. These assets must be in compliance with FAA. The concerned PFI would be required to submit (for Refinace) all the details of the asset including its valuation, etc., which will be duly validated in the term sheet at the time of sanction have been fully complied with.

The above will ensure that only new/additional projects (which were not already support by PFIs before launch of the programme) are covered under the programme.

AE acknowledges the positive feedback from ITAP on the sustainable-development potential of the FMAP proposal.

# ITAP feedback (Point 40): Funding green technologies in MSMEs is currently perceived as risky. This flexible approach allows lenders to provide financing that is fit for purpose. However, it should be noted that the availability of concessional funding may not keep up with market needs.

# SDHIB Response: The programme has been designed on the blended finance concept to offer concessional loans to MSMEs either directly or indirectly through the PMF (NBFC/SFB/MFs). The programme will create an ecosystem for financing climate technologies and will provide a platform for MSMEs to access concessional loans. The programme intends to create an ecosystem of climate financing which would allow them to lend to the MSMEs with an increased understanding of the associated risks and benefits. The blended finance concept is also designed in such a way that it sets an example for the PMFs to follow successfully lending to the MSMEs for adopting low emission climate change technologies.


---
**Page 120**
---

This will be achieved by:

(i) Offering concessional finance to MSMEs directly encouraging them to adopt relevant climate-smart technologies and interventions. However, at the same time minimum energy savings of 10% will be ensured on a sub-loans basis and 20% at the programme level.

(ii) Offering concessional financing for MSMEs to purchase green labelled products to FVs which would help in reducing their risk of lending towards MSMEs thus enabling them to offer additional financing.


The programme has also built suitable checks and balances which includes M&E on sample basis of sub-loans, periodic reporting of the climate benefits and passing on the concessional benefit to the borrower. This will ensure that the FVs are not getting windfall profits without any climate benefits. The programme proposes to work with not one FV but two FVs in each state. It is expected that competition among these FVs for the same MSMEs would be sufficient competition among these FVs for climate financing. This would obviate the risk of windfall profits to the FVs and shall ensure further benefits to the ultimate borrowers like MSMEs.


As regard independent evaluation, it may be mentioned that the programme has suitable provisions for interim and final evaluations through independent third-party agencies.

Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

**FMAP Feedback (Page 87):** However, the FMAP had noted some areas of concern, primarily related to the operationalisation of EMAP over time. Some of these concerns are strengthened are noted throughout this assessment. SIDBI submission follows the following:

1. SIDBI’s total balance sheet size is around USD 57 billion (FY 2018). Out of the above around 90%, approximately USD 53.4 billion is towards provisioning on lending/advance. Refinance support to 50 Banks, 70 NBFCs, 4 SPVs/MFs. This depicts SIDBI’s strong relationship with banks and NBFCs. SIDBI has a very strong track record in terms of its large exposure to climate financing. SIDBI has been actively pursuing energy efficiency financing in MSMEs and is currently supporting over 150 projects under the Green Credit Scheme for MSMEs. The present outstanding of direct green loans is USD 85 million as on March 31, 2014. Hence, AE has strong capacity to undertake loans as well as to encourage MSMEs and the FVs to participate in the programme.

2. It may be mentioned that there are more than 63 million MSMEs in India and the programme is targeting only around 10,000 MSMEs. Notwithstanding the above,

# GENDER ANALYSIS & ASSESSMENT STUDY

## 1.1 INTRODUCTION

In the past few years, India has been increasingly faced with the challenge of mitigating the detriments of and adapting to the adverse impact of climate change and balancing the same against the need for sustainable development. The increasing demand for energy resources, energy systems and infrastructure, concomitant with the needs of a vast and rapidly developing country, climate related vulnerabilities are now in desperate need of attention.

Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (“MSMEs”), forming a vital part of the Indian economy and playing a critical role in the sector of employment generation in the country, today face considerable operational challenges, energy costs being one of the key constraints. The lack of access to finance, coupled with inadequate access to credit and investments, as well as limited financial, technical knowledge and capacities further render MSMEs vulnerable to adverse impacts of climate change.

It is in this context that SIDBI, India proposes to address such deficiencies, through a programme called “Financial Management Assistance Project (FMAP) in MSMEs”, that is aimed at facilitating MSMEs to become more climate-resilient, sustainable and competitive in the long run.” (“Programme”) This Programme will be upscale ongoing projects, through affordable and accessible finance and technical support for projects pertaining to climate change mitigation and adaptation projects.

Among other aspects, the design of the programme takes into consideration, the existing gender issues, and concerns, in conformity with international norms and national priorities.

### 1.1.1 RATIONALE OF THE ASSESSMENT

The Gender Assessment will help to understand relationship between men and women, their access to resources, their activities, and the constraints they face relative to each other.

The objective of the present document is to showcase findings basis a Gender Assessment Study and formulate a Gender Action Plan for the Programme, with respect to interventions that would be related to MSMEs. The Programme would be related to mitigation specific interventions such as Energy Efficiency/Technology upgradation, Electric Mobility, Renewable Energy (including Solar). Further, the interventions will also include adaptation specific interventions

1


---
**Page 126**
---

across a variety of themes including Climate Smart technologies, Water, Wastewater, Innovative Technologies etc.

The scope of work under the Programme entails, conducting a gender assessment study, developing a gender action plan, as well identifying the gender impact and benefits accruing from the said programme.


### 1.1.2 METHODOLOGY NOTES

The assessment and the action plan under the Programme is prepared in alignment with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Gender Policy, in consonance with the instructions provided by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), and in line with the GCF’s commitment to aligning its programmes with international agreements and normative frameworks. The exercise of acknowledging disproportionate gender impact, aids in examining the inter-sectoral constraints and issues pertaining to women entrepreneurs and their enterprises. This is aimed at incorporating a course to enhance the 'agency' of women in the change process (in contributing to mitigation and adaptation activities, enhancing resilience and well-being, supporting livelihoods in volume and diversity, etc.).)

It is to be noted that GCF has placed gender perspectives as a "key element of its programme architecture" and deems it an "essential decision-making element for the deployment of resources towards a thoroughly 'gender-responsive' approach for its projects/programmes.

In preparing the Gender Action Plan for the Programme, following approaches have been leveraged:

1. Baseline assessment of participation of women in the MSME sector in India as entrepreneurs
a. The literature reviewed from secondary sources to understand the baseline situation and the broad scope for EE applications in the sector considering gender perspectives. In this regard, relevant reports, about national policies and guidelines on energy efficiency (EE), historical studies and research on Energy Efficiency (EE), international best practices, potential EE interventions etc. considering gender equity were examined. The references are provided in Annexure A of Appendix B.
b. This assessment has been done based on SIDBI's existing initiatives to promote women entrepreneurship and based on research studies supported by SIDBI.
c. SIDBI in collaboration with Dun and Bradstreet introduced a quarterly Sustainability Perception Index (SIDBI & D&B SPPI®) - the green pulse


---
**Page 127**
---

indicator: SPNVC is a pioneering effort to assess the sustainability perceptions of MSMEs across various industries and locations. The index is designed to provide a comprehensive overview of the sustainability landscape in the Indian MSME sector.

### Assessment of challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in the MSME sector

#### 2. Stakeholder consultation i.e., "Strengthening Green Financing and Market Access for Gender Equality" through the Government of India Financial System (GIFS)™ - "Greening of Finance by Women (Grow)" Initiatives, founded by SIDBI, Shakti Sustainable Energy Foundation (SSEF), and the Ministry of Women and Child Development (MoWCD). The proceedings of stakeholder consultation attached at Annexure III.

#### 3. Review of SIDBI's existing capabilities to promote women entrepreneurship/gender equality

- a. This assessment has been done based on a review of SIDBI’s existing initiatives for women entrepreneurship and participation of women in the MSME sector.
- b. Further, SIDBI's gender policies have been reviewed in line with GCF's gender policies.

The above assessments feed into the Gender Action Plan proposed for the Programme and the specific outcomes from the Gender Action Plan for the Programme.


### 1.2 BACKGROUND AND CONTEXT: GENDER IN INDIA

#### Gender and Employment in India – Data Trends
There remains no doubt in the growing potential of women in terms of their economic participation. It is to be noted that the difference between the overall literacy rate for adult males and females aged 15 and above have significantly reduced, with the current participation of women estimated at 65.79 per cent of the total female population, against the male population comprising of 82.37 percent.

However, it is pertinent to note that there was a significant disproportionate gender impact because of the COVID-19 pandemic in sectors of labour, employment, financial inclusion and with reference to other socio-economic considerations. In the


---
**Page 128**
---

global context, the WEF Gender Gap Report 2021[1] ("WEF Report 2021") highlighted the following pointers:

- 5 per cent of all employed women lost their jobs, compared with 3.9 per cent of employed men (ILO projection)
- marked decline of women's hiring into leadership roles across multiple industries
- increase in care work for women.
- automation, digitalization as compounding factors in 'labour market disruption' – with implications for 'gender parity' further in terms of jobs and occupational gender segregation.

The WEF Report 2021 further identifies the increased likelihood of gender gaps in sectors that require discrete technical skills. Further, in fields where women are under-represented, they are likely to face discrimination and other institutional or social barriers in job transition, particularly in emerging areas of growth potential, with widening skill gaps.

In case of India, reports furnished by UN Women (2021)[2] and Centre for Sustainable Employment, Azim Premji University (2021)[3] point to certain alarming trends that threaten the national and international investments in gender empowerment and long-term gain for women. The pandemic has further exacerbated the plight of the pandemic-affected large section of the workforce without access to employment or social protection. These reports also identify the increased informality and decline in earnings for majority of women with women and younger workers disproportionately affected, because of the pandemic.

For women, it is observed that wage inequality and the burden of unpaid care has pushed more women out of employment falling into depths of poverty, with women losing jobs during COVID-19, and therein engaging more in unpaid care work, both in India and globally.

This section attempts to provide an overview of gender-related workforce allocation vis a vis the MSME sector in India. Given the scope of the present study, the focal point of this section is to furnish national level data, in respect to the participation of women as workforce in the economy and the current scenario of women workforce and ownership via a vis the MSME sector.

# 1.4 CHALLENGES RELATED TO WOMEN ENTREPRENEURSHIP IN MSME SECTOR IN INDIA

The MSME sector in India contributes to ~30% of India's GDP and employs around 110 million people. In India, about 89% of the women-owned enterprises operate all year round, 5% are seasonal, and the remaining 2% operate casually, which are neither regular nor seasonal.

The stakeholder consultation highlights the efforts needed to support the greening of Indian financial systems and the role of MSMEs in initiatives scaling up of renewables, energy efficiency, electric vehicles and sustainable business. The event brings together stakeholders from different sectors to share learnings and drive sustainable practices. Women entrepreneurs are a key part of India's economy and workforce, with women-owned enterprises playing an important role in the green and climate finance sector. Women entrepreneurs share their experiences and insights in sustainable business, highlighting the challenges they face and the potential solutions.

Following are some of the key challenges faced by women entrepreneurs in India:

- **Not Sustainable:** ~73% women-led enterprises have faced a reduction in revenues post COVID-19. Most female entrepreneurs are concentrated in sectors and industries highly affected by the pandemic and digitization like micro, small, and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs), service providers, shop owners, dressmakers, shoekappers and street vendors, arts and handicrafts vendors, and beauty-parlor owners, among others. This points to the fact that women-led enterprises are not financially sustainable/resilient enough to recover from external shocks.

- **Not Autonomous:** In ~20% cases, the business-related decisions are taken by family members other than the entrepreneur herself or himself running the business. Often, women do not truly own or control many women-led enterprises. They depend considerably on men for key business decisions, procurement of raw materials, pricing information, and market access.

- **Not Formal:** ~95.6% of the women entrepreneurs in India are informal and not registered with relevant national/state institutions. A high level of informality harms the health and resilience of their enterprises and their entrepreneurial rights. The level of effort involved in running such enterprises goes unaccounted.


---
**Page 133**
---

for and expose women to occupational health hazards. Further, informality leads to challenges in accessing finance, market, etc.

- **Not sufficient employment generating**: ~85% of women proprietors run with less than six workers, most being own-account or single person enterprises.
- Only ~1–9% proprietors run with six to 10 employees. This points to the fact that women are underrepresented in scaling businesses, especially in areas such as business management, market access, access to finance.

### Lack of awareness and skills on green jobs and technologies: Women in Indian MSMEs have been confined to low-paying, low-skilled jobs. The ongoing transitions present them with an opportunity to realign their skill sets and adjust to the new realities. Digital literacy is one of the most important skills that women need to acquire to keep pace with the ongoing transitions. This will enable them to enter traditionally male-dominated sectors such as those heavily on digital technologies, such as healthcare services, automobiles, and more advanced roles within the textiles sector with green technologies.

Currently, there is limited evidence on how climate change affects women in the MSME sector. The impact of these disruptions presents both a challenge and an opportunity for mainstreaming gender inclusion and empowerment through the proposed project.


#### 1.5 GENDER LENS ON MSMES AND CLIMATE (MITIGATION & ADAPTATION)

The role of women entrepreneurs in driving inclusive economic growth cannot be ruled out. Women entrepreneurs not only generate income themselves but also benefit their communities, thereby creating a ripple effect of positive socioeconomic impact. By fostering entrepreneurship opportunities for women, we can reduce gender disparities in employment and help alleviate poverty. Targeted interventions to address the specific needs of women in different regions and sectors, especially in clean energy and climate-resilient agriculture, would be the best next step.

Women entrepreneurs are not just agents of change; they are the architects of a sustainable future. To create a more inclusive, equitable, and sustainable future for generations to come, women's endeavours must be supported. Further, an enabling environment should be created for women to flourish and share their learning’s and insights while tackling climate change.

A recent report on "improving women's productivity and incomes" through energy/climate initiatives has also been conducted in 2021 by the Council on


---
**Page 134**
---

Energy, Environment and Water. Citing a direct link or contribution to SDGs 5, 7 and 8, women’s engagement as producers, customers, and suppliers in this area is seen to have potential for expanding markets and achieving scale.

Opportunities:

In order to address women's specific and systemic vulnerabilities, there is a need for collective efforts that support women in the sector.
• There is a need for national and state level governments to ensure effective skill training in addition to training provided by the employer.
• Drawing from India’s experience, Indian women are also more susceptible to economic fluctuations and other shocks that affect the MSME sector. For instance, during the first lockdown in 2020, only 7 per cent of men lost their jobs, compared with nearly 45 per cent of women. In the formal sector, women had to work by the end of the year. In the informal sector, women faced even worse. Between March and April 2021, rural Indian women in informal jobs accounted for 85 per cent of those who lost their jobs.
→ "Indian women also spend more time doing unpaid care work at home than men. On an average, they spend 6-8 times more time than men on unpaid care work, which includes cooking, cleaning, and looking after elders and the sick. During the pandemic, their share of unpaid care work grew by nearly 30 per cent." Centre for Sustainable Employment, State of Working India (2021)
→ "More girls than boys were left out of school during the pandemic and 65 per cent of parents surveyed were reluctant to continue the education of girls and send them back to school because of cost. This can create an entire generation of young women without education and employment opportunities." UN Women (2021)

As women-led businesses continue to recover from the pandemic-induced shocks, there is an emerging need to adopt cleaner energy technologies, digitalization, and other transitions in the value chain. As these transitions necessitate new skills and capabilities, they further compound the existing challenges that women in MSMEs currently face.
• New skills and training for women in green jobs: Women are more inclined to embrace technologies and mechanisms that offer cost and energy savings, leading to increased cash flows. It is imperative to develop suitable business cases and models to promote the adoption of renewable technologies, such as solar energy and biogas. There is, therefore, a need to make such innovations


---
**Page 135**
---

accessible to female entrepreneurs through training and research provisions. It is crucial to educate women about the long-term implications of climate change for their businesses and highlight the potential opportunities arising from the adoption of clean energy and climate-resilient practices. Additionally, on-the-job training programs should be designed to develop relevant skill sets among female workers. These skills include renewable energy technologies, financial management, digital and information technology, leadership, and management. This approach will not only support female workers in making informed choices when selecting new jobs but also enable them to contribute to their professional development and decision-making capabilities.

* **Establishing Institutional Support:** Women workers and entrepreneurs face significant barriers that prevent them from accessing sectors and industries traditionally dominated by men. Ensuring that women are not left behind during the low-carbon transition and placing them at the forefront of climate adaptation and mitigation efforts is crucial for a just transition. Creating opportunities for women requires innovative strategies and policies that effectively address social, cultural, and economic biases. The formation of networks among women in different sectors and industries and taking on new and multiple roles is vital. Even as women enter sectors such as aviation, engineering, and construction, they require targeted support and skills for technological innovation.

* **Gender-responsive financing for MSMEs:** Access to credit is a primary barrier for women in MSMEs. Improving access to formal and affordable financial services can serve as a catalyst in supporting women during the transition to low-carbon economies. Financial inclusion for women can lead to social and economic empowerment by improving access to a range of financial products, including savings, credit, insurance, and payment services. Many female-owned MSMEs operate as unregistered enterprises and continue to face issues in accessing formal financing to meet their business requirements. The credit needs of women entrepreneurs are often different from those of men due to cultural and legal factors.

* **Promote decent and dignified workplaces for women:** Business sustainability involves striking a balance between social, economic, and environmental considerations. From a gender perspective, this implies providing conducive and safe working conditions, access to resources, training, and career development opportunities that are tailored to the needs of women across different life stages, including marriage and motherhood. There is a need for innovative and flexible mechanisms that facilitate and encourage an effective return to work.

The current mechanisms for accessing climate funds offer ad hoc solutions. There is a need to move towards systems approaches that provide financial assistance and


---
**Page 136**
---

offer support to entrepreneurs in sectors undergoing multiple transitions, such as low- cartoon and technological transitions. It is crucial to consider systemic benefits and institutional mechanisms for a just transition.

In this regard, encouraging women entrepreneurs in the "green" economy represents an opportunity to make women-led enterprises more resilient and sustainable, thus enabling a 'just' transition through FMAP.


# 1.6 INITIATIVES TAKEN BY GOVERNMENT TO PROMOTE & SUPPORT WOMEN ENTREPRENEURSHIP IN MSME SECTOR IN INDIA

There are several Government focused legal regulations in India, that aim to address the vulnerabilities and cultural barriers associated with gender, through the provision of certain measures that support a safe and equal professional platform for women. These regulations are presented below.

| Equal Remuneration Act, 1973 | provides for payment of equal remuneration to men and women workers for the same work or similar work in establishments employing both sexes |
| --- | --- |
| The Maternity Benefit Act, 1961 & The Maternity Benefit (Amendment) Act, 2017 | regulate employment of pregnant women in certain establishments for a certain period before and after childbirth and provide for maternity and other benefits |
| The Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013 | has been enacted to prevent sexual harassment against women in the public and private sector, whether organized or unorganized |

The G20 New Delhi Leaders' Declaration 2023 focuses on "Enhancing Economic and Social Empowerment," "Bringing the Gender Digital Divide," "Driving Gender Inclusion," "Empowering Women for Peace, Prosperity, Nutrition, and Well-Being," Creation of a Working Group on the Empowerment of Women.

The Government of India had launched programmes like Beti Padhao, Beli Bachao campaign (launched in 2015) for improving the Gender Parity Index (GPI). It is by promoting survival, protection and education of girl child. The scheme aims to address the issue of female Child Labour and gender discrimination targeting the underlying social mind set and creating awareness about the criticality of the issue. Furthermore, Mahila E-Rast has been launched by the Government in 2016 for promoting women entrepreneurs with the objective to provide an e-marketing platform by leveraging technology for showcasing product manufactured/sold by women entrepreneurs/SHGs/NGOs.

# Gender Parity Index (GPI) in education is a valuable indicator which reflects the discrimination against girls in access to educational opportunities


---
**Page 137**
---

Further, The Ministry of MSME, Government of India has taken a number of steps to promote and support women owned-MSMES<sup>1</sup>

1. Special drives have been undertaken for registration of women owned MSMEs under the URP.
2. In order to endorse women entrepreneurs, the Public Procurement Policy was amended in 2018 by the Ministry of Commerce/Department. Undertakings to procure at least 3 per cent of the annual procurement from women-owned MSMEs.
3. For further support women entrepreneurs under the Credit Guarantee Scheme for MSMEs, the following two provisions have been introduced for w.e.f. 01.12.2022:
   a. 10 per cent concession in annual Guarantee fees; and
   b. 10 per cent additional Guarantee coverage of up to 85 per cent, as against the 75per cent for other entrepreneurs.
4. In order to encourage entrepreneurship among women, the Ministry of MSME has launched the Skill Upgradation & Mahila Cor Yojana' under 'Cor Vikas Yojana', which is an exclusive training program aimed at the skill development of women artisans engaged in the Coir sector.
5. The Prime Minister's Employment Generation Programme (PMEGPT), which is a major credit-linked subsidy programme aimed at generating self-employment opportunities through establishment of small units and enterprises, has been extended to women entrepreneurs.
6. A higher subsidy is being provided to beneficiaries belonging to special categories such as Scheduled Castes/Scheduled Tribes/Other Minorities/women or economically weaker sections in the rural and semi-rural areas.
7. For purposes of encouraging participation of women entrepreneurs in trade fairs under the Procurement & Marketing Support Scheme, a higher subsidy is provided for their participation.

8. "SAMARTH" initiative was launched to support women owned MSMEs with the objective of providing Skill Development and Market Development Assistance to women entrepreneurs engaged in various sectors including those in rural and sub-urban areas. Aspiring and existing women entrepreneurs have been provisioned with 20 per cent seats in free Skill Development Programs organized by the Ministry of MSME. Women entrepreneurs are also provided 15 per cent of MSME Business Delegations for domestic & international exhibitions under the schemes for Marketing Assistance implemented by the Ministry of MSME; and 20 per cent discount on annual processing fee on National Small Industries Corporation's Scheme.


<sup>1</sup><i>https://pub.gov.in/ProcurementframePage.aspx?PRID=2003574</i>


---
**Page 138**
---

9. MSME Sustainable Zero Defect Zero Effort (ZED) Certification Scheme is an initiative to provide a roadmap to global competitiveness for Indian MSMEs having economic and social impacts in terms of capacity building of MSMEs. The aim of the scheme is that the goods being manufactured by the MSME sector have zero rejects and at the same time has zero effect on environment. Under this scheme, support is provided to women entrepreneurs; there is a provision of 100 per cent subsidy on cost of ZED Certification for Women owned MSMEs.

10. Under the broader composition of MSME Innovative Scheme, a special MSME Idea Hackathon 3.0 has been organized for women entrepreneurs wherein 18,886 ideas have been received yet.

The Ministry of MSME implements the following capacity building programmes for capacity building and skill enhancement for MSMEs, including women entrepreneurs in MSMEs:

- Entrepreneurship Skill Development Programme ("ESDP"): The objective of ESDP is to promote new enterprises, to build capacity of existing MSMEs and to inculcate entrepreneurial culture in the country. About 40 per cent of the targeted beneficiaries of ESDPs are stated to belong from weaker sections of the society (SC/ST/PH/BPL). A subsidy of Rs.15,000 per head is provided; no participation fee will be charged from SC, ST, Physical Handicapped, Below Poverty Line (BPL) participants and women.

- Mahila Shakti Yojana (MSY): It is a programme for empowering women artisans in the co-sector by providing these women with a stipendary two-month training programme. These women artisans are encouraged to avail assistance under Prime Minister's Employment Generation Programme (PMEGP).

These initiatives are expected to spur the growth of women entrepreneurs in the MSME sector in India.

# SIDI B'S EXISTING INITIATIVES/MECHANISMS TO SUPPORT WOMEN ENTREPRENEURS IN MSME SECTOR

Small Industries Development Bank of India (SIDBI) plays a proactive role in the promotion and development of Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) to address various financial and non-financial challenges faced by them. It aims at innovating for wider impact and to benefit MSMEs in both 'setting up' and 'stepping up', thereby promoting entrepreneurship culture.

Swavalamban aims at promoting the aspiring, livelihood, micro and small entrepreneurs. SIDBI promotes entrepreneurship culture and encourages women, youth and rural entrepreneurs. Swavalamban is a flagship programme of SIDBI which connects rural migration to urban areas and promotes sustainable livelihood opportunities with thrust on the bottom of the pyramid, unserved/underserved pockets/ segments of Micro and Small Enterprises.

SIDBI has been undertaking several impactful interventions for promotion of entrepreneurship and development of enterprises at the grass root level. The Programmes for Development & Impact (PDI) Vertical of the Bank through its Swavalamban programme provides (up to) 100% support from FDCD, UK aims to promote inclusive and innovative entrepreneurs in towns/cities pockets of India, with a special focus on women entrepreneurs.

## Institutional Capacity of SIDBI

SIDBI has been a pioneer in supporting women-led MSMEs in India. SIDBI has created and nurtured (for 25+ years) its microfinance ecosystem in India. For example, the first pro-poor support microfinance institutions had created 150 micro-finance institutions to support women's self-help group (SHGs). This has reached 30-40 lakh women – this segment has grown such that 9 Institutions have evolved to small finance banks (e.g. Bandhan, Utkarsh).

SIDBI aims at promoting financial inclusion and entrepreneurship for women. SIDBI aims to support 20% of its financing towards led MSMEs.

Following are some of the key programmatic initiatives undertaken by SIDBI to further this cause!

* **Jal Sparsh-Waste to Wealth:** The uncontrolled growth of water hyacinths is a problem in many water bodies in the country. It blocks waterways and limits boat traffic, recreation, flood control and wildlife use. It produces a dense canopy at the water surface, and the plant shade limits native submerged plant species and can also lead to eutrophication. That's why in 2013, the Water Hyacinth Trust (WHT) Foundation floated a solution to make productive use of the uncontrolled growth of water hyacinths, by its extraction from the water bodies. The solution has also


---
**Page 140**
---

helped the local women earn sustainable and meaningful livelihoods. Hence, the intervention has a positive effect on the environment as well as the livelihood. The rural women were provided with skills to sustainably extract the water hyacinth vines and the methods to process it to form useful products such as bags, mats, stands, and other decorative. The activity mainly engages poor women for the entire year. The project has been implemented in 5 states of India through various outreach online and offline avenues such as e-listing on commerce platforms, exhibitions, and fashion shows in schools. The project is present in 5 states in India.

- **Women Entrepreneurship - Livelihood Initiatives on Green Energy and Rural Households Electrification (WE-LIGHT):** Climate change is a massive issue to address and can be mitigated by using solar energy. Non-electricity and limited access to power supply further adds to the problems. It is an integrated approach to address climate change and poverty reduction through a solar-based livelihood initiative that empowers women in rural areas, making them self-sufficient and resilient. The knowledge and utility of solar is thus transferred to the community for sustainable access to electricity at an affordable cost for the rural poor. The project aims to provide training on business planning and skills to effectively deploy capital that delivers impacts for the environment, women, communities, and the rural poor. It brings together the most holistic program in India with a focus on design and marketing, sales, marketing, entrepreneurship, and leadership which allows rural women to have access to capital to scale the distribution of renewable energy products in the rural market in India.

- **Prayas Initiative:** In FY 23, loans upto Rs. 5 Lakhs for livelihood activities were extended to ~80,000 micro enterprises, most of which are women led.
- **ARJANA:** This plan includes various financial benefits such as reduced credit guarantee fees, no prepayment charges, additional advantages for ZED

certification, discounts for timely repayment, lower processing fees, and lower promoter margins (only) for women entrepreneurs).
* Swavalamban Initiative: SIDBI's Mission Swavalamban has collaborated with Usha International Limited to promote women entrepreneurship in underprivileged areas through the Swavalamban Silar (i.e. Tailoring Homespuners Programme). This has helped over 30,000 marginalized women become Silar Homespuners in 25 states over 5 phases.
* SWAS: SIDBI and 'Mukti' collaborated to launch the 'Swavalamban Accelerator in Sundarbans'. The project aims to empower women by providing alternative livelihoods and rebuild the Sundarbans, which was affected by Super Cyclone Amphan. 1000 women were trained in food processing and handicrafts (500 each) and received seed capital of Rs.2000/- per woman. As a result, 417 beneficiaries established new microenterprises and 771 beneficiaries were provided with market linkage.
* SMART: SIDBI's Smart Village Support Programme India is working to create 200 model villages in Western and Central India, with a focus on training tribal youth, especially women, in micro-enterprise development. Under this program, the goal is to train 5000 women entrepreneurs by FY 2023-24. As of FY 2023, 275 people have been trained and 200 have started their own enterprises.
* WE-LEAD: The project being carried out by OSFII in Eastern India aims to support 12,000 women entrepreneurs, amongst SHG/ JLG members, by providing them with training and access to microfinance and government services. In FY 2023, 7,970 women were trained, 2,859 women were given credit, and 2,852 enterprises were established. The project is replicated in partnership with the Government of Odisha for the purpose of promoting 10,000 women entrepreneurs.
* TOWER: Mahabubnagar-Khamman Unagari Sundresh is implementing the TOWER project to empower tribal women entrepreneurs in a district in Eastern India by upskilling their businesses using non-triber forest products. A modern machinery powered by solar energy has been established to produce high-quality Saal leaf plates and bowls, which are sold at a premium price.
* Empowering Women and Enhancing Their Business through E-Mobility (EWEE): The rural India is critical for the nation's development and the women are the backbone of this sector. They need to be empowered with greater opportunities to build the nation. One of the many ways to empower women is to improve the mobility options in rural parts to encourage them to start their businesses, job opportunities, etc. SIDBI launched E-WEE project to ease the mobility of women entrepreneurs by providing them with a loan and blended financial facility to purchase electric two-wheelers with the support of Non-Banking financial companies.
* SIDBI is a signatory of the signature of Finance in Common (FIC) gender coalition to the Paris Development Banks Statement on Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment.

**Page 142**
---

Beyond these programmatic initiatives, SIDBI has undertaken several structural reforms/adopted policies to promote women’s welfare and entrepreneurship.

* **Shrinking of women employee base & equal opportunity employer:** As of FY23, SIDBI had 17% women employees (vs. 89% men). This indicates that SIDBI is an equal opportunity provider and has implemented fair practices for enabling career progression. For example, in FY 23, SIDBI made 1607 nominations for various positions across India, with 45% of the nominations being women employees (~20% of overall nominations).
* **Gender Policy:** As a GCF accredited National Implementation Entity (NIE), SIDBI has adopted a Gender Policy at organizational level (attached below). This policy governs SIDBI’s organizational policies with respect to gender equality and inclusion. Further, the policy also governs SIDBI's engagement with the private sector in terms of gender equality.
* **Further:** SIDBI is mandated to comply with Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013
* **Further:** Gender-related considerations have been factored in the EAS checklist that many financial products are based on.

Based on the above information, SIDBI is adequately placed to implement gender awareness activities. SIDBI has already implemented multiple schemes encouraging women entrepreneurship across different geographies in India. The previous experience of these schemes will be leveraged for the implementation of the Gender Policy/Action plan and ensure effective monitoring & reporting. For FAMP, SIDBI will employ suitably qualified Gender experts.

3. GENDER ACTION PLAN

### Establishing the Context/ Baseline to the Action Plan

The present proposal prioritizes the mainstreaming of gender concerns, with focus on leveraging the funding to improve gendered coverage of programme financing and benefits. The immediate goal of the programme is to see an 80:20 split (80% financing towards women), broadly in line with the selected baselines for women's ownership and labour force participation. In the longer term, the aim is to contribute to a paradigm shift in the MSME sector by promoting women's leadership/ management and technical roles/workforce participation in, as well as other benefits from climate mitigation and adaptation activities taken up in the MSME sector in India.

As a critical support to MSME financing and development in the country, SIDBI is also called upon to provide momentum for creating an enabling environment, contributing to public stakeholder awareness, as well as identifying and setting operational and regulatory standards to this end.

The overall direction of recommended actions to this end would be in terms of:
- Building on existing mechanisms and provisions supporting financial inclusion and increasing women's access to finance through SIDBI as a vehicle enhancing their agency in climate mitigation and adaptation initiatives through this programme.
- Collaborating with existing industry associations and women entrepreneurs/ other networks on targeted capacity-building programmes on women and energy efficiency, resource management.

In addition, and particularly relevant for an institution such as SIDBI, given its niche status in the MSMEs sector are aspects of strengthening the enabling environment for gender mainstreaming. These pertain to issues such as:
- operational learning to sustain gender mainstreaming efforts
- technical assistance outreach
- sector-wide awareness campaigns, capacity building and entrepreneurship development programmes
- innovations as gender sensitive credit ratings, support with registration and paperwork, etc

### Mapping of Proposed Gender-Sensitive Programme Measures Against Programme Cycle and Project Activities


---
**Page 145**
---

Based on the programme design, the broad aim is to ensure adequate representation for women as stakeholder and beneficiary under component (1) supporting affordable financing of mitigation and adaptation activities; targeted technical support under component (2), for capacity development, awareness generation, etc., as well as in M&E frameworks; and leveraging financing mechanisms for greater and more equitable access to climate change finance covering gender aspects.

Specific, gender sensitive programme measures can then be given by the following categories:

* Screening Parameters for Beneficiary MSMEs
* Actions by Performance Indicators for SIDBI
* Incentives
* SIDBI Niche Enablers

In the case of screening parameters, the suggestion is to give weightage and priorities to:

(i) sectors/geographies with higher representation of women (top 10 states with women entrepreneurs/women-led MSMEs as per Table 12 or 13 of this document), as well as to
(ii) firms owned by women in those with significant representation in the management or work force. These weightages would ideally translate into/align with a points system on eligibility criteria, and link with a higher degree of concessionality in financing terms as well as other support services provided beyond credit.

The aim, however, is to include MSME clusters comprising women-led enterprises and/or with significant representation, as eligible beneficiaries under the programme. This would be in line with the Government's commitment to extend programme benefits and impacts given the current distributions in this regard, and to secure advance beyond given targets.

While appraising the cases for MSMEs, certain commitments/undertakings would be required as a pre-condition for providing financial assistance such as:

1) Availability of a gender policy or action plan under the policy of SIDBI.
2) Existence of committee to perform Grievance redressal for the cases pertinent to gender based violence (GBV), Sexual Exploitation Abuse and Harassment (SEAH) as per the (POSH, Sexual Harassment of Women at Workplace (Prevention, Prohibition and Redressal) Act, 2013.

**Actions by SIDBI**

In the implementation of the programme in line with its core functions and operating context would essentially include bringing together its gender inclusive provisions.

# OUTCOMES FROM PROGRAM

## Reskilling women workers who are already employed in the MSME to adopt, adapt, and apply best practices and providing relevant skills to women entering the workforce is essential to ensure sustainability. Increasing women's share in MSME sector is essential to harness equitable economic gains and facilitate social transformation at scale.

It is important to ensure that women-led MSMEs are better equipped in the context of climate change and low-carbon transition. The overarching goal is to equip them with the necessary capabilities—the key skills required for green jobs, access to finance, and essential technologies—thereby disrupting the traditional occupational segregation of women in the sector and transitioning to a sustainable and just economy.

Ensuring decent and dignified workplaces for women business sustainability involves striking a balance between social, economic, and environmental considerations. From a gender perspective, this includes ensuring equal pay, safe working conditions, access to resources, training, and capacity building for female workers.

The programme aims to significantly transform the landscape of women led MSMEs in India. The following are the outcomes that the program aims to achieve:

- **Number of women led MSMEs:** Increase the number of women owned MSMEs through necessary interventions and ensure enhanced participation of women entrepreneurs.
- **Business Sustainability through improve access of finance:** Make women feel financially secure by providing them with access to affordable loans, especially climate shocks by providing access to affordable finance for mitigation and adaptation technologies. Services and technologies provided by the programme will help women-led MSMEs in adopting new business models.
- **Autonomy:** Increased skilling for women entrepreneurs to ensure their businesses are sustainable. The programme provides hands-on support on the adoption of better technology, Encourage women entrepreneurs to engage in energy efficiency, renewable, electric vehicles and other identified sectors.

- Encouraging gender-focused practices among MSMEs: The project would encourage those MSMEs with women-centric workplace or are willing to create the same. This will include having a gender action plan, separate rest spaces, better lighting, etc.
