# Gender Policy

## Introduction

Gender equality and female empowerment are now universally recognized as core development objectives, fundamental for the realization of human rights, and key to effective and sustainable development outcomes. No sector including the micro, small and medium enterprises (MSME) sector can be successful in achieving this objective and increasing the distribution of opportunities, resources, and choices for females and males so that they have equal responsibilities and ability to shape their own lives, make choices and contribute meaningfully towards the economic growth and development.

India apex financial institution, SIDBI has been taking pioneering initiatives for women's financial inclusion and economic empowerment through some its initiatives.

## Need for Gender Policy

* As we know, MSME sector is the backbone of the Indian economy with a strong impact on creating jobs, stable income and reducing poverty. Further, it has largely been observed that one of the underlying causes of poverty, inter alia, can be attributed to women's very limited access to opportunities and resources. Gender inequity is a key part of this.

* Gender discrimination cannot be simply eliminated by recruiting more women; it requires sustained efforts across all levels of the organization and end users and critical mass of support for further inclusion through organizational culture and its work.

* SIDBI's pioneering role in nurturing the microfinance sector in the country has been incorporated into the National Micro, Small & Medium Enterprises Policy 2018 with the aim achieving inclusive growth. SIDBI aligns with the constitutional mandate of Government of India for providing equity and equality, as testified in SIDBI’s Code of Conduct which prohibits any discrimination against any person or group to discriminate on the basis of age, race, gender, marital status, place, religion or disability.

* Promotion of gender equality and environment protection are the two major pillars of SIDBI's policy framework towards sustainable development. The Article provisions under Constitution of India are provided in Box 1.


---
**Page 154**
---

# Box I: Constitution of India provisions

* Article 14: Men and women to have equal rights and opportunities in the political, economic, social and cultural fields.

* Article 15(1): Prohibits discrimination against any citizen on the grounds of religion, race, caste, sex or place of birth.

* Article 15(3): Special provision enabling the State to make affirmative action for women and other backward classes.

* Article 18: Equality of opportunities in matters of public appointments for all citizens.

* Article 24(a): The State shall direct its policy towards securing all citizens men and women equal opportunity for participation in the national economy, social and cultural life.

* Article 39(b): Equal pay for work done by both men and women.

* Article 39(d): Equality of status and opportunities to men and women. Conditions at work and maternity relief.

* Article 41: Right to work, leisure and play as a part of the practice derogatory to the dignity of women.

A policy is needed to reflect a changing environment where it is imperative to take appropriate measures for equitable growth (i) codify SDBE's commitment to gender equality and sustainable development goals; (ii) increase women empowerment and capacity building, including access to financial services; (iii) mainstream gender considerations in banking culture and operations with regard to women.

Currently, SDBE is accredited as National Implementing Entity (NIE) of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) of the UNFCCC. Development of a Gender Policy was one of the key recommendations of GSBM at NIE to GCF.

### 3. Objective of the Policy

The objective of the policy is to promote gender equality, women empowerment, capacity building of men and women for gender equity, gender responsive financial services and to be equal in all spheres of life. The policy will also help SDBE to implement strategies to mainstream gender equity in SDBE's policies and operations.

### 4. Broad Framework of the Policy

Gender Policy of SDBE is based on five principles:

#### 4.1 Equity and Equality:
a. Promote equal rights, opportunities, power and responsibilities and organizational structures and functions of SDBE (gender equality);
b. Promote equal and fair access to credit (gender equity) in terms generating activities.


---
**Page 155**
---

### 4.2 Inadequeness: Work towards reducing social and economic barriers, addressing issues of wage discrimination against women, productive use of time, equal access to and control over productive assets and opportunities including institutional credit, rights-based approaches in economic, commercial etc., and promoting gender-responsive budgeting.

#### 4.3 Promoting women's economic empowerment through financial inclusion and entrepreneurship

Actively contributes to furthering women's financial inclusion and entrepreneurship through the following ways: (a) Focusing on microfinance services for women and a focus on the most marginalized women (b) Funding innovative start-ups by women entrepreneurs, particularly in the areas of renewable energy, agribusiness, clean tech, biotechnology and innovation with credit plus approach to reduce gender gaps and empower women through financial inclusion (c) Promoting green finance which lays that gender responsive, green, clean and energy efficient enterprises are given priority lending support to women entrepreneurs to start and scale up their enterprises.

### 4.4 Access to Information

Making institutions more sensitive for delivery of non-discriminatory projects and programs to achieve progress without any gender discrimination.

### 4.5 Alignment with national policies and priorities

SBI aligns its policies, strategies, and operations with the national policies and priorities of the Government of India for all the activities, projects / programmes related to gender mainstreaming.

## Policy Governance & Organisation role

### 5.1 SDBI's mandate

In terms of Government of India’s policy as applicable to public financial institutions, SDBI shall provide opportunities for all women staff members, good working conditions for personnel growth, equal opportunity for promotion, capacity building and career development, and ensure that the gender balance is maintained at various levels, and affirmative decision in favour of women officers in transfers. Accountability mechanisms, human resource systems and policies are made gender-sensitive and responsive.

### 5.2 Financial Intermediaries / Executing Entities

(i) SDBI enforces its financial intermediaries/executing entities to promote gender equality and inclusion in all their activities and projects. The policy will be implemented through the following ways: (a) All financial intermediaries/ executing entities will create a sustainable framework for promoting women's economic empowerment. Suitable conditions would be stipulated in the Memorandum of Understanding / MoU of the financial intermediaries / executing entities for overall implementation of this policy.

(ii) Financial intermediaries/executing entities will be responsible for overall implementation of various covenants of Gender Policy under the projects assisted with the funds from GOI.


---
**Page 156**
---

### Resource Allocation

SDBI through its various promotion & development activities allocates financial assistance to projects / programmes. SDBI may also handhold the financial intermediaries implementing projects / programmes. The cost of the project / programme will be shared by both staff and/or through consultants, and the cost for the same will be a part of the project / F&A budget.

### Operational Guidelines

SDBI may modify/amend its operational guidelines under various developmental and business activities, wherever required, to include:

a) The projects / programmes should endeavor to have specific gender components both in physical and financial terms; provide for project templates and review criteria, effective monitoring and evaluation tools, and ensure that the projects / programmes include benefits and approaches for men and women.

b) Gender mainstreaming may be incorporated into SDBI's outreach activities and a suitable communication strategy may be developed to spread knowledge on gender equality and gender-sensitive development.

c) Gender assessment & gender action plan, wherever required (at entity level, fund level and project level), should be prepared as part of the project / programme design/development projects for creating base-line data and promoting adequate participation of women in such projects.

d) Gender-response procedures and requirements, gender assessment and action plan guidelines, gender-sensitive monitoring & evaluation tools, and gender-sensitive and gender-responsive indicators etc., at the entity level, fund level and at the project level should be developed and incorporated into SDBI's approved projects/programmes referring to GCF updated gender assessment and action plan and gender strategy of GCF.

### Review Mechanism

Based on gaining experience in the implementation of the gender policy activities and operational modalities, SDBI will be able to align its policies, processes, procedures, and project / programme design with the GCF's gender policy. SDBI may review the policy once in a two (2) years.

# GIFS-GROW ANNUAL EVENT

The GIFS Initiative is the first Indo-European platform which supports the greening of the Indian financial system, through knowledge exchanges, training, and networking, targeting like-minded finance practitioners, policymakers, regulators, and financial institutions. The GIFS Initiative expects to widen and shape the discourse around greening the financial ecosystem and support its operationalization through the Indian institutions; it has been founded by Shakti Sustainable Energy Foundation (SSEF) in 2019 with the support of the State Bank of India (SBINL) in December 2021. In 2022 and 2023, more than 500 participants participated in the different events organized by the GIFS Initiative. GIFS gathered again the financial community to reflect on the recent developments on green finance from the financial market side and invited more stakeholders to discuss how to ensure a resilient and low-carbon transition for MSMEs (Micro, Small & Medium Enterprises) can find a common ground to ensure a resilient and low-carbon transition in India. The conference also included the GriWt summit. GriWt is the professional network for women in green and climate finance, which was created as part of the GIFS Initiative.

## OBJECTIVES OF THE ANNUAL EVENT

- Platform to share updates on the 2022 and 2023 activities (newly created regulations, schemes, tools, and methodologies) that have contributed to the greening of the Indian financial system.
- GIFS visibility: capitalization and presentation of its next developments, which include (i) setting up the "Greening the Finance-to-Finance Green" (G2FG) Technical Assistance platform for MSMEs and financial institutions; and (ii) network for Greening of Finance by Women (GriWt).
- Networking of the financial and MSME communities
- Present key updates, enable collaboration and advancing engagement of women in green and climate finance through GriWt

# INTRODUCTION

The GfS-GroW Annual Summit’s opening session, themed “Visions on GroW by Women Entrepreneurs,” featured a female climate entrepreneur focused on sustainability. The session had Ms. Chakta Ahuja of Brown Living, who is operating an innovative plastic- and chemical-free packaging company, and Dr. Anita Prasad of REV Environmental Solutions that focuses on waste reduction and resource reuse. Ms. Kavya Srinivasan of Agri-Tech Innovations aimed to transform the sector of agricultural technologies (agri-tech) and add value to each step of the crop cycle: soil, water, manure, or farm produce. The goal is to create a people- and earth-centered approach to create zero-packing footprint products. In the session, they shared their transformative journeys and obstacles faced while running environmentally friendly businesses. They discussed the achievements and challenges that define the landscape of eco-conscious companies in the present world. The session highlighted the unique difficulties faced by these pioneering women entrepreneurs, ranging from brand recognition and balancing ecological impact with financial sustainability. All of this occurred within the context of a worldwide trend towards sustainable practices.

As the conversation unfolded, a common thread emerged on the pervasive funding gap for women-led green and climate enterprises. This disparity is challenging established investment paradigms to nurture and propel women entrepreneurs striving to make meaningful contributions toward environmental solutions. The session provided a reassessment of investment strategies and a shared commitment to backing enterprises genuinely devoted to advancing global sustainability.

## Key Insights

### Funding Disparity in Sustainability
Studies show that female founders receive a disproportionately small amount of total start-up funding, with only a slight increase observed over the years. However, if start-ups with both male and female co-founders are considered, there is an improvement in funding received, although it remains below 20%. These findings indicate the need to address the gender gap by providing adequate support and resources for female entrepreneurs. Overcoming this challenge requires a fundamental shift in investment practices, recognizing how women entrepreneurs play in driving innovative and sustainable solutions.

### Language Barrier Between Innovators and Investors
The need for a shared language between innovators and investors in the environmental sector was emphasized. Bridging this communication gap is vital for translating and scaling innovative green business models. Enhancing mutual understanding fosters an environment where environmentally impactful innovations align seamlessly with financial sustainability.

### Technological Solutions for Agri-Tech
Potential of agri-tech was showcased with innovative IoT-based solutions addressing significant post-harvest waste. Traditional storage practices often lead to significant losses


---
**Page 161**
---

ranging from 40-50% in the agricultural sector. One transformative example is gas monitoring technology that detects spoilage in grain silos where wastage is identified by smell; this IoT-based electronic device detects it at a much earlier stage, improving early identification from 20% to over 90% of cases. This demonstrates the revolutionary power of technology in reshaping the agri-tech storage ecosystem.

### Educating Investors on Sustainability

Women entrepreneurs face challenges in educating investors on the unique aspects of sustainability. Overcoming this hurdle is crucial for dispelling misconceptions and garnering support. As more women enter the process, there's a growing need for targeted initiatives, workshops, and platforms that facilitate direct interactions between women entrepreneurs and investors. Establishing mentorship

| GFS-Growth Annual Event – Conference Proceedings |

programs and industry-specific training sessions can bridge the knowledge gap and foster a supportive environment for sustainable ventures. By actively promoting such educational avenues, the ecosystem can create a more informed and supportive environment for women entrepreneurs pursuing sustainability-driven initiatives.

### Role of Platforms in Increasing Visibility

Entrepreneurs expressed gratitude for platforms like GrowIt, recognizing them as essential for visibility, networking, and access to resources. These platforms play a pivotal role in advocating for increased funding for women-led initiatives, fostering an enabling environment, and effectively raising awareness about the impactful work of female entrepreneurs in a predominantly male-dominated sector.

# SESSION 2: POLICY PERSPECTIVE ON CLIMATE FINANCE LANDSCAPE INDIA: DEVELOPMENTS IN THE BANKING SECTOR, AND REGULATION FOR THE SAME

## PANELISTS
- Mr. Sivasubramanian Ramann
Chief Managing Director (CMD), SIDBI
- Mr. Sunil Nair
Chief General Manager, Reserve Bank of India (RBI)
- Mr. V. Chandrasekar
Senior Advisor, Indian Banks' Association (IBA)


---
**Page 163**
---

# INTRODUCTION

The second session at the GIFS-GroW Annual Summit brought together esteemed speakers to address the pivotal role of policy perspectives in navigating the evolving landscape.

## Key Insights

### Financial Challenges in Electric Vehicle (EV) Adoption

The challenges hindering the economic viability of EVs are manifold. Despite India's monumental aspiration of achieving 30% of new sales of electric vehicles by 2030, only 5 million out of the projected 160 million units have been realized. The financial obstacle lies in the fact that EVs cost 1.5 times that of Internal Combustion Engine (ICE) vehicles, coupled with high-interest rates on loans. This financial landscape poses hindrances to consumers and necessitates a reevaluation of financing structures for the adoption of EVs more economically viable.

### RBI's Green Deposits Framework

The Reserve Bank of India (RBI)'s response to the evolving landscape with the introduction of a green deposits framework in April 2023 is highlighted. The aim was to steward India's promise towards a framework that ensures funds raised under green deposits are genuinely directed to green initiatives. The framework defined criteria for raising and deploying funds, emphasizing the need for a financing framework and third-party verification. It showcased how a clear regulatory framework encourages responsible lending practices, mitigating the risk of greenwashing.

### Incentivizing Green Deposits and Capital Incentives

The focus on incentivizing green deposits and exploring differential interest rates for such deposits was a focal point. Complexities emerged concerning capital maintenance requirements, liquidity management, and potential penalties or incentivization based on lending to green or non-green sectors. The ongoing discourse revolves around determining the ideal capital structure for these financial activities. The Basel Committee is actively exploring possibilities to strike a balance between risk and incentivization in the realm of green finance.

The central bank has not mandated or endorsed any differential interest rates to be implemented at this time. The Basel Committee is reportedly in the process of discussing


---
**Page 164**
---

integration of climate factors into Pillar 1, 2 and 3, but it may be too early to make any changes to the capital framework for green and climate projects. As more knowledge is gained about the risk levels associated with different green and climate projects, supervisory approaches will need to evolve. However, it was emphasized that banks should not avoid financing transition and climate projects, as they are important in building client capacity to support these activities.

**IBA's Advocacy Role in Green Finance**

The Indian Banks' Association (IBA) was recognized for its pivotal role in advocating green finance. As an unregistered association, IBA champions the cause of banks, collaborating with international organizations and conducting seminars to enhance awareness. The focus is on integrating climate considerations into a comprehensive framework, emphasizing the importance of taxonomy, disclosure, and scenario analysis for making green investments. IBA has been active in task forces related to the greening of steel and the National Green Hydrogen Mission showcased its commitment to steering the banking sector towards sustainable practices.

## Challenges in Greening MSMEs

The challenges in introducing green technologies to the Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs) sector were underscored. The fragmented and often entrepreneurial nature of MSMEs requires continuous handholding, awareness-building, and simplifying government processes. IBA has made efforts to streamline processes under the Ease of Doing Business initiative and introducing clusters to comprehensive schemes from various ministries were discussed. The importance of providing MSMEs with a holistic package of schemes to effectively transition towards sustainable practices.

## Facilitating Energy Efficiency in Clusters

Discussions focused on facilitating a comprehensive framework for support available for MSMEs to adopt energy-efficient measures seamlessly. This aligns with the broader objective of promoting ease of doing business and promoting sustainable practices in the MSME sector. An example is the establishment of the Facilitation Centre for Energy Efficiency (FCE) as a flagship initiative. This center aims to provide a consolidated platform for MSMEs, offering information on technologies, financing options, and government schemes.


---
**Page 165**
---

# INTRODUCTION

The third session of the GIFS-GROW Annual Summit provided a nuanced exploration of the evolving landscape where climate action, Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), and gender inclusivity converge. The distinguished speakers, including Mr. Nitin Desai—the Board Member of the Green India Financial System Foundation; Mr. Sivasubramanian Ramanan, the CMD of SIDBI; and Ms. Audrey Brule, the Deputy Head of the Financial Systems Division from the World Bank—discussed various pathways for sustainable finance, the pivotal role of MSMEs in climate resilience, and strategies to integrate green finance into climate and financial sectors. This session encapsulated a robust dialogue on aligning financial mechanisms with environmental goals, propelling MSMEs beyond networking into tangible action, and the imperative of integrating gender-focused initiatives into the broader climate agenda.

The session, over and above all tried to underscore the initiatives undertaken by the Green Indian Financial System. The proposition of the GIFS (Greening the Finance-to-Finance Green Transition) was also platformed as a catalyzing force for foster collaboration.


## Key Insights

### MSMEs: Beyond Networking to Action

There was a demand for MSMEs to move away from networking and instead focus on taking action. This involved combining technology, entrepreneurship, and finance, paralleling the success of the Silicon Valley; this insight emphasized the need for a holistic ecosystem supporting MSMEs in climate change mitigation, innovation, and sustainability.

### Role of Finance in Climate Resilience

A concluding case study, spotlighting the impact of finance in transitioning traditional coal-based furnaces to gas-based ones in Moradabad, a city in the state of Uttar Pradesh, was underscored.

Gender-Centric Climate Finance Strategies

Challenges and Opportunities in Climate Financing

# INTRODUCTION

The session on lessons learned from G20 Presidency for the Indian Financial sector & COP28 messages at the GPS-GROW Annual Summit 2023 in New Delhi aimed to dissect global trends in green finance and chart out strategies for an effective transition.

Moderating the discussion as part of the Green Climate Finance Vertical at SIDBI, the panel included Mr. Dhruba Puriyakastha, the Niti Aayog’s Chief Climate Policy Officer (CPO), Mr. Vivek Aditya, the Head of Climate & Sustainability at the Boston Consulting Group (BCG); Ms. Neha Kumar from the Head of South Asia Program at the Climate Bonds Initiative; Mr. Shubhashis Dey, the Director of Climate Policy Program at the Shakti Sustainable Energy Foundation; and Mr. Mahesh Chandrasekaran from Cube Investment. This dynamic discussion aimed at evaluating COP28 outcomes, analyzing the impact of global green finance and additionally, outlined critical challenges and opportunities, emphasizing the need for precision, collaboration, and innovative financing mechanisms in the pursuit of sustainable transitions.

## Key Insights

### Global Climate Finance Dynamics

The global climate finance landscape, particularly regarding international finances was discussed. Despite effective communication strategies, the lack of quantitative targets in phasing out fossil fuels and the commitment to triple renewable energy was deemed insufficient, especially considering the negligible pipelines in several countries, emphasizing the need for mobilization of international resources for a global public good.

### Mathematics of Transition Finance

The session delved into the fundamental mathematics of transition finance, where light was shed on the cost disparity of mitigating one unit of CO2 emissions between developed and developing nations. The failure of COP28 to recognize this distinction raises questions about the equitable utilization of international resources. This underscores the significance of accurate mathematical models and international collaboration in achieving sustainable transitions.

## Challenges in Climate Communication

Drawing parallels between COP28 and earlier climate summits, the communication challenges in conveying transitional fuel nuances were discussed. The panel highlighted the importance of effective communication, avoiding the oversimplification of complex concepts. This insight resonates beyond climate conferences, accentuating the significance of effective communications for climate action initiatives and garnering global support.

## ESG Governance in MSMEs

The importance of incorporating Environmental, Social, and Governance (ESG) principles in micro, small, and medium enterprises observed in larger companies, such as recognition-focused committees, circularity, internal carbon pricing, and Business & development (BBD), was highlighted. The challenge of MSMEs lies in accessing technology and financing, where institutions such as SIDBI could play a pivotal role by showcasing


---
**Page 172**
---

exemplary MSMEs and providing additional support.

**Facilitating a Just Transition**

The necessity of a rapid, orderly, and just transition, acknowledging that decarbonization is critical for achieving climate goals, was discussed. While corporations have taken the lead, it was noted that the financial sector's delayed entry needs acceleration. The session underscored the importance of addressing this gap at global summits, advocating for increased accessibility and affordability of capital between developing countries.

---

**Role of Private Capital and Blended Finance**

The evolving role of private capital in addressing climate aspects was acknowledged, indicating the need for innovative instruments. Blended finance emerged as a potential solution, but the emphasis shifted to converting projects into bankable propositions. Technical assistance was identified as crucial for project viability, ensuring the successful conversion of projects and attracting philanthropic funds for impactful market-making initiatives.

# INTRODUCTION

The sixth session of the GIFS-GROW Annual Summit brought together a distinguished all-women panel to explore the intersection of women and climate change with diverse stakeholders. The session heard insights from Ms. Ekta Mehra, Senior Sector Specialist at KfW; Ms. Smriti Agarwal, Director at IIFCL; Mr. Shridh Srivastava, Investment Director at Avanza Capital. It was moderated by Ms. Netu Orikhara, Managing Director and Partner at Intellecap.

## Blending Climate and Gender in Financing

The effective integration of climate and gender perspectives in financing initiatives can yield positive outcomes, especially in sectors like urban mobility and energy. Urban mobility projects, for example, can be designed with a gender lens, enhancing workforce participation rates for women. However, challenges persist in scaling up necessary tailored approaches for blending climate and gender considerations.

## Data Challenges in Gender-Related Metrics

Obtaining gender-related data for reporting and analysis poses a significant challenge despite reporting standards emphasizing gender metrics. The importance of conversations on reporting gender-related information was highlighted. Building internal capacities and creating structures supporting gender-friendly policies are essential to navigate the complexities of data collection.


| Investment Philosophy and Gender Mainstreaming |
| --- |
| Mainstreaming gender in investment processes involves considerations like sensitization, flexible post-maternity policies, and intentional efforts to include women on boards. These considerations can eliminate biases in investment processes; it has been increasingly observed that companies with teams that are women-friendly and supporting women founders through mentorship and networking. |

During a panel discussion, a proposal was made for SIDBI to build a gender and climate-focused platform. The panelists also discussed the financial parameters necessary for such initiatives with a specific focus on gender and climate objectives. Perspectives were shared on how the challenges faced with supporting women in the workforce could be offset through philanthropic blended capital. They also highlighted the potential of initiatives like setting up daycare facilities and proposed framing these costs as R&D investments to capture tax and other advantages. While on the other hand, concerns were raised about global competition for funds and questions why companies would invest in such


---
**Page 175**
---

# Role of Blended Finance in Gender and Climate Initiatives

Blended finance, involving concessional finance, plays a crucial role in supporting gender and climate initiatives. However, aligning financial and social parameters poses challenges. The panel highlighted that while blended finance offers support for women's empowerment, it is essential to align expectations of financial and gender experts. Sustainability-linked products and strong dialogues between borrowers and lenders are deemed effective.

## Performance Impact of Women Borrowers

Studies suggest that financial products perform better when women are borrowers, emphasizing the importance of reporting mandates and investor focus on gender metrics. The potential advantages of reporting mandates, where investors prioritize gender metrics, acting as a driving force for institutions to utilize gender-related information effectively are significant.

## Future Expectations and Aspirations

The session concluded with aspirations for a future where women and men share equal vulnerability to climate change, leading to an overall reduction in vulnerability, particularly for economically vulnerable sections. The panel envisioned a future with increased integration.
